<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{Def:NuclearGelfandPair}
The couple $({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}')$ that is formed by a topological vector space ${\mathcal{S}}$ and
its topological dual ${\mathcal{S}}'$ is called a \emph{nuclear Gelfand pair} if
\begin{enumerate}
\item ${\mathcal{S}}$ is nuclear and Montel
\cite{Grothendieck1955,Pietsch1972,Treves2006};
\item there exists a continuous and dense embedding $\iota:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ that is
canonical, in the sense that
$\langle\iota\varphi,\psi\rangle=\langle\iota\psi,\varphi\rangle$ for all
$\varphi,\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$.
\end{enumerate}
Unless stated otherwise, ${\mathcal{S}}'$ is endowed with its weak-$\ast$ topology. This
choice is harmless because the Montel property ensures that a weak-$\ast$ convergent
sequence in ${\mathcal{S}}'$ converges strongly as well.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{Def:HilbertGelfand}
Let $({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}')$ be a nuclear Gelfand pair. A Hilbert space ${\mathcal{H}}$ is called an
\emph{${\mathcal{S}}$-Hilbert--Gelfand space} if
\begin{align*}
{\mathcal{S}} \overset{d}{\hookrightarrow} {\mathcal{H}} \overset{d}{\hookrightarrow} {\mathcal{S}}',
\end{align*}
where the notation ``$\,\overset{d}{\hookrightarrow}\,$'' denotes a continuous and dense embedding.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{Def:CondPositiveDef2}
Let ${\mathrm{A}}$ be a continuous operator ${\mathcal{S}} \to {\mathcal{S}}'$ and ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ a
finite-dimensional subspace of ${\mathcal{S}}'$ that is spanned by ${\boldsymbol{p}}=(p_1,\dots,p_{N_0})$.
The operator ${\mathrm{A}}$ is said to be
\begin{itemize}
\item symmetric or self-adjoint if, for all $\varphi_1,\varphi_2 \in {\mathcal{S}}$,
$\langle {\mathrm{A}} \varphi_1,\varphi_2\rangle=\langle {\mathrm{A}} \varphi_2,\varphi_1\rangle$;
\item positive-definite if, for any $\varphi \in {\mathcal{S}}\backslash\{0\}$,
$\langle {\mathrm{A}} \varphi,\varphi\rangle > 0$;
\item positive-semi-definite if, for any $\varphi \in {\mathcal{S}}$,
$\langle {\mathrm{A}} \varphi,\varphi\rangle \ge 0$;
\item ${\boldsymbol{p}}$-conditionally positive-definite if
\begin{align}
\label{Eq:PositiveDefinite}
\langle {\mathrm{A}} \varphi,\varphi\rangle > 0
\mbox{ for all }\varphi \in {\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}} \backslash\{0\},
\end{align}
where ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}:={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}^\perp
=\{\varphi \in {\mathcal{S}}: {\boldsymbol{p}}(\varphi)={\boldsymbol{0}}\}$.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{Def:CompatibleRieszPair}
Let $({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}')$ be a nuclear Gelfand pair, and let ${\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ be
continuous positive-definite operators. We form the Hilbert spaces
\begin{align*}
{\mathcal{H}}=\overline{({\mathcal{S}},|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{A}}})},
\qquad
{\mathcal{H}}'=\overline{({\mathcal{S}},|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}})}.
\end{align*}
The pair $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}})$ is a \emph{compatible Riesz pair} for $({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}')$ if ${\mathrm{A}}$ and
${\mathrm{B}}$ extend to mutually inverse isometric isomorphisms
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{A}}:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}',
\qquad
{\mathrm{B}}:{\mathcal{H}}'\to{\mathcal{H}}.
\end{align*}
When this holds, ${\mathrm{B}}$ acts as the inverse of ${\mathrm{A}}$, which justifies the notation
${\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{A}}^{-1}$ that we use in the sequel.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{Theo:HilbertPositiveDef}
Let $({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}')$ be a nuclear Gelfand pair and let ${\mathcal{H}}$ be a Hilbert space that contains
${\mathcal{S}}$ as a dense subspace. Then, the following statements are equivalent.
\begin{enumerate}
\item ${\mathcal{H}}$ is an ${\mathcal{S}}$-Hilbert--Gelfand space; i.e.,
${\mathcal{S}} \overset{d}{\hookrightarrow} {\mathcal{H}} \overset{d}{\hookrightarrow} {\mathcal{S}}'$.

\item The continuous dual ${\mathcal{H}}'$ is an ${\mathcal{S}}$-Hilbert--Gelfand space;
i.e., ${\mathcal{S}} \overset{d}{\hookrightarrow} {\mathcal{H}}' \overset{d}{\hookrightarrow} {\mathcal{S}}'$.

\item The Hilbert space ${\mathcal{H}}$ is continuously embedded in ${\mathcal{S}}'$:
${\mathcal{H}} \overset{}{\hookrightarrow} {\mathcal{S}}'$.

\item There exists a compatible Riesz pair $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{A}}^{-1})$ for
$({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}')$ (see Definition~\ref{Def:CompatibleRieszPair})
such that
\begin{align*}
\|\varphi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}
=
|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}
=
\sqrt{\langle {\mathrm{A}}\varphi,\varphi\rangle},
\qquad
\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}.
\end{align*}
\end{enumerate}
Whenever these conditions hold, the pair in Item 4 is unique and is
identified as
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{A}}
=
{\mathrm{J}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}|_{{\mathcal{S}}},
\qquad
{\mathrm{A}}^{-1}
=
{\mathrm{J}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{-1}|_{{\mathcal{S}}},
\end{align*}
where ${\mathrm{J}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}'$ is the underlying Riesz map;
moreover,
\begin{align*}
\|\varphi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}'}
=
|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}^{-1}},
\qquad
\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}.
\end{align*}
Conversely, every compatible Riesz pair $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{A}}^{-1})$
generates a Hilbert--Gelfand dual pair
\begin{align*}
({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}')
=
\Big(
\overline{({\mathcal{S}},|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{A}}})},\;
\overline{({\mathcal{S}},|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{A}}^{-1}})}
\Big),
\end{align*}
where the Riesz map ${\mathrm{J}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}'$ is the unique
continuous extension of ${\mathrm{A}}$.
Moreover, the canonical duality pairing for $({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}')$ is
compatible with the pairing for $({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}')$. Specifically, for
every $f\in{\mathcal{H}}'\subset{\mathcal{S}}'$ and every
$\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}\subset{\mathcal{H}}$,
\begin{align}
\label{Eq:DualityProdH}
\langle f,\varphi\rangle
=
\langle f,\varphi\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}'\times{\mathcal{H}}}.
\end{align}
By continuity, \eqref{Eq:DualityProdH} extends to every
$(f,g)\in{\mathcal{H}}'\times{\mathcal{H}}$, with
\begin{align}
\label{Eq:HDualityBound}
|\langle f,g\rangle|
\le
\|f\|_{{\mathcal{H}}'}\,\|g\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}.
\end{align}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{Cor:AlgebraicInverse}
Let ${\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ be continuous positive-definite operators with Hilbertian extensions $\widetilde{{\mathrm{A}}}$ and
$\widetilde{{\mathrm{B}}}$ on the complete spaces ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ and ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$. Then, the following statements are equivalent.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}})$ is a compatible Riesz pair for $({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}')$
(Definition~\ref{Def:CompatibleRieszPair}).
\item[(ii)] ${\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{A}}=\mathrm{Id}$ on ${\mathcal{S}}$ \emph{through Hilbertian
extensions}; equivalently,
the Hilbertian extensions $\widetilde{{\mathrm{A}}}$ and $\widetilde{{\mathrm{B}}}$ are mutually inverse Riesz
maps.
\item[(iii)] The two norms are dual to one another on ${\mathcal{S}}$:
\begin{align*}
|\psi|_{{\mathrm{B}}}=\sup_{\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}\backslash\{0\}}
\frac{|\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle|}{|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}}
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}=\sup_{\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}\backslash\{0\}}
\frac{|\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle|}{|\psi|_{{\mathrm{B}}}}.
\end{align*}
\end{enumerate}
When these hold, ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ and ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}={\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}'$ are Hilbert--Gelfand spaces,
and ${\mathrm{B}}$ is the unique operator that inverts ${\mathrm{A}}$, which justifies the notation
${\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{A}}^{-1}$.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
We establish (ii)$\Leftrightarrow$(iii) and (i)$\Leftrightarrow$(iii).

\medskip
\emph{(ii)$\Rightarrow$(iii)}:
Let us assume that $\widetilde{{\mathrm{A}}}$ and $\widetilde{{\mathrm{B}}}$ are mutually inverse. Then
$\widetilde{{\mathrm{B}}}=\widetilde{{\mathrm{A}}}^{-1}$ identifies ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ isometrically with the dual
${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}'$, so that $\|{\mathrm{A}}\varphi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}=|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ for every
$\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}$. Since $\widetilde{{\mathrm{B}}}$ extends ${\mathrm{B}}$, the Riesz identity reads
$\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle=\langle{\mathrm{A}}\varphi,\psi\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}$ for every
$\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$; as ${\mathcal{S}}$ is dense in ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$, the supremum over $\psi$ gives
$\sup_{\psi\neq0}|\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle|/|\psi|_{{\mathrm{B}}}=\|{\mathrm{A}}\varphi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}
=|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}$, which is the second identity of (iii). The first follows from the symmetric
computation with $\widetilde{{\mathrm{A}}}\widetilde{{\mathrm{B}}}=\mathrm{Id}$.

\medskip
\emph{(iii)$\Rightarrow$(ii)}:
The identities of (iii) are the relations \eqref{Eq:CompatBound}--\eqref{Eq:CompatSharp} in the proof of Theorem \ref{Theo:HilbertPositiveDef} with
${\mathrm{A}}^{-1}={\mathrm{B}}$. By Step 2 of the proof of Theorem~\ref{Theo:HilbertPositiveDef}, they realize
$\widetilde{{\mathrm{A}}}$ and $\widetilde{{\mathrm{B}}}$ as mutually inverse Riesz maps.

\medskip
\emph{(i)$\Leftrightarrow$(iii)}:
If $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}})$ is a compatible Riesz pair, then Step 1 of the proof of
Theorem~\ref{Theo:HilbertPositiveDef}, applied to ${\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$, produces
\eqref{Eq:CompatBound}--\eqref{Eq:CompatSharp} in the appendix with ${\mathrm{A}}^{-1}={\mathrm{B}}$, which is (iii).
Conversely, (iii) supplies these relations, which are the sole ingredients of Step 2 of that proof:
they realize ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ and ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}={\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}'$ as mutually dual
Hilbert--Gelfand spaces and identify $\widetilde{{\mathrm{A}}},\widetilde{{\mathrm{B}}}$ as the unique continuous
extensions of ${\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}}$; hence $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}})$ is a compatible Riesz pair.

\medskip
\emph{Uniqueness}:
Let us suppose that $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}}_1)$ also satisfies (iii). The first supremum gives
$|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}_1}=|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ on ${\mathcal{S}}$, so that ${\mathrm{B}}_1$ and ${\mathrm{B}}$ induce the same
Hilbert--Gelfand space ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}'$; the uniqueness part of
Theorem~\ref{Theo:HilbertPositiveDef} (Step 6) gives ${\mathrm{B}}_1={\mathrm{B}}$.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{Rem:AutomaticSymmetry}
The symmetry of ${\mathrm{A}}$ is not required as a hypothesis anywhere in this work, the reason being
that it is a consequence of the Riesz-pair property. Let $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}})$ be a compatible Riesz
pair, let ${\mathrm{J}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}'$ be the Riesz map of ${\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$, and let
${\mathrm{T}}:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}'$ be the isometric extension of ${\mathrm{A}}$ that
Definition~\ref{Def:CompatibleRieszPair} provides. Then,
${\mathrm{U}}:={\mathrm{J}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{-1}{\mathrm{T}}:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}$ is an isometric isomorphism, and the
compatibility \eqref{Eq:DualityProdH} of the pairings gives
$\langle{\mathrm{U}} u,u\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}=\langle{\mathrm{A}} u,u\rangle=\|u\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}^2$ for $u\in{\mathcal{S}}$,
hence for every $u\in{\mathcal{H}}$ by density. Consequently,
\begin{align*}
\|{\mathrm{U}} u-u\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}^2
=\|{\mathrm{U}} u\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}^2-2\langle{\mathrm{U}} u,u\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}+\|u\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}^2
=0,
\end{align*}
so that ${\mathrm{U}}=\mathrm{Id}$ and ${\mathrm{T}}={\mathrm{J}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}$. This yields
$\langle{\mathrm{A}}\varphi,\psi\rangle=\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}$ for all
$\varphi,\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$, which is a symmetric expression. The operator ${\mathrm{A}}$ is therefore
symmetric, and it is the unique operator that induces ${\mathcal{H}}$ (Step 6 of the proof of
Theorem~\ref{Theo:HilbertPositiveDef}). The same argument applies to the ${\boldsymbol{p}}$-conditional Riesz
pairs of Section~\ref{Sec:CondRieszPairs}, where it yields the symmetry of ${\mathrm{A}}$ on
${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ and the uniqueness of the conditioned operator
${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ of Theorem~\ref{Thm:Concretization}, while ${\mathrm{A}}$ remains unconstrained off
${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
\label{Def:AttainableNull}
Let $q$ be a continuous seminorm on ${\mathcal{S}}$. Its \emph{attainable null space} is
\begin{align*}
{\mathcal{N}}_q:=\big\{p\in{\mathcal{S}}':\ p=\textstyle\lim_{k}\chi_k\ \text{in}\ {\mathcal{S}}'
\ \text{for some}\ (\chi_k)\subset{\mathcal{S}}\ \text{with}\ q(\chi_k)\to0\big\}.
\end{align*}
For the seminorm induced by a positive-semi-definite operator ${\mathrm{B}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'$, we write
${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}:={\mathcal{N}}_{|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}}}$.
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
\label{Def:Intrinsic}
Let ${\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ be continuous, with ${\mathrm{B}}$ symmetric positive-definite and
${\mathrm{A}}$ ${\boldsymbol{p}}$-conditionally positive-definite, so that the Hilbert spaces ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ and
$\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ of \eqref{Eq:HA}  and \eqref{Eq:HB}  are well defined. 
Then, the pair $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}})$ is a \emph{${\boldsymbol{p}}$-conditional Riesz pair} for $({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}')$ if
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$; and
\item[(ii)] the map $\pi\,{\mathrm{A}}$ and the operator ${\mathrm{B}}$ extend to mutually inverse isometric
isomorphisms
\begin{align*}
\pi\,{\mathrm{A}}:\ {\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}\ \longrightarrow\ \dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}},
\qquad
{\mathrm{B}}:\ \dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}.
\end{align*}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{Prop:Realization}
Let $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}})$ be a ${\boldsymbol{p}}$-conditional Riesz pair in the sense of
Definition~\ref{Def:Intrinsic}. Then, the following properties hold.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Duality bound}: For every $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ and $\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$,
\begin{align*}
|\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle|\le|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}\,|\psi|_{{\mathrm{B}}},
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle=\langle\pi{\mathrm{A}}\varphi,\pi\psi\rangle_{\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}} .
\end{align*}
\item \emph{Realization of ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$}: Let $h\in{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ be represented by the
$|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{A}}}$-Cauchy sequence $(\varphi_j)\subset{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$. Then the limit
\begin{align*}
\langle h,\psi\rangle:=\lim_{j\to\infty}\langle\varphi_j,\psi\rangle
\end{align*}
exists for every $\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$, depends only on $h$, and defines an element of ${\mathcal{S}}'$. The
resulting map ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ is linear, injective, and continuous for the weak-$\ast$
topology; it realizes ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ as a subspace of ${\mathcal{S}}'$.
\item \emph{Realization of $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$}: In the same fashion, every $g\in\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$
that is represented by the $|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}}$-Cauchy sequence $(\psi_k)\subset{\mathcal{S}}$ defines an
element of $({\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}})'\cong{\mathcal{S}}'/{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ through
$\langle g,\varphi\rangle:=\lim_k\langle\psi_k,\varphi\rangle$ for $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, and
the resulting map $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'/{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ is linear, injective, and
continuous.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
\emph{Duality bound}:
Let $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ and $\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$. Clause (ii) provides a
$|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}}$-Cauchy sequence $(\psi_k)\subset{\mathcal{S}}$ that represents $\pi{\mathrm{A}}\varphi$ and
satisfies ${\mathrm{B}}\psi_k\to\varphi$ in ${\mathcal{S}}'$. Therefore
\begin{align*}
\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\langle{\mathrm{B}}\psi_k,\psi\rangle,
\qquad
|\langle{\mathrm{B}}\psi_k,\psi\rangle|\le|\psi_k|_{{\mathrm{B}}}\,|\psi|_{{\mathrm{B}}},
\end{align*}
where the inequality is the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality for the ${\mathrm{B}}$-form. In the limit,
$|\psi_k|_{{\mathrm{B}}}\to\|\pi{\mathrm{A}}\varphi\|_{\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}=|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}$, where the last
equality is the isometry property of Clause (ii). This proves the bound. The same computation
identifies the pairing: the ${\mathrm{B}}$-form extends continuously to $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$, which gives
$\lim_k\langle{\mathrm{B}}\psi_k,\psi\rangle=\langle\pi{\mathrm{A}}\varphi,\pi\psi\rangle_{\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}$.

\medskip
\emph{Realization of ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$}:
The bound of Item 1 gives
$|\langle\varphi_j-\varphi_i,\psi\rangle|\le|\varphi_j-\varphi_i|_{{\mathrm{A}}}\,|\psi|_{{\mathrm{B}}}$, so that
the scalar sequence $(\langle\varphi_j,\psi\rangle)_j$ is Cauchy and its limit vanishes for every
null sequence: the limit exists and depends only on $h$. In the limit,
$|\langle h,\psi\rangle|\le\|h\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}}\,|\psi|_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ for every $\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$. Since
${\mathrm{B}}$ is continuous, $|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ is a continuous seminorm on ${\mathcal{S}}$, so that
$\langle h,\cdot\rangle\in{\mathcal{S}}'$; the same bound makes the realization map weak-$\ast$ continuous.
For the injectivity, let us suppose that $\langle h,\psi\rangle=0$ for every $\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$, and
let ${\mathrm{J}}$ denote the isometric extension (Riesz map) of $\pi{\mathrm{A}}$ to ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$. The
identity of Item 1 passes to the limit and gives
$\langle h,\psi\rangle=\langle{\mathrm{J}} h,\pi\psi\rangle_{\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}$, so that the class
${\mathrm{J}} h$ is orthogonal to $\pi({\mathcal{S}})$, which is dense in $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$. Hence
${\mathrm{J}} h=0$ and, by the isometry, $h=0$.

\medskip
\emph{Realization of $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$}:
The preceding argument applies with the roles of $({\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}},|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{A}}})$ and
$({\mathcal{S}},|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}})$ exchanged: the bound of Item 1 makes the limit $\langle g,\varphi\rangle$
exist and depend only on $g$, with
$|\langle g,\varphi\rangle|\le\|g\|_{\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}\,|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}$, and the continuity of
${\mathrm{A}}$ gives $\langle g,\cdot\rangle\in({\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}})'$. Two points are specific to this side.
First, the dual $({\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}})'$ identifies with ${\mathcal{S}}'/{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, because
${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}^\perp={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ is finite-dimensional, hence weak-$\ast$ closed. Second, the
injectivity follows from
$\langle g,\varphi\rangle=\langle g,\pi{\mathrm{A}}\varphi\rangle_{\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}$ and from the
density of $\pi{\mathrm{A}}({\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}})$ in $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$, which holds because $\pi{\mathrm{A}}$ is an
isometric isomorphism and ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ is dense in ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{Thm:AttainableNull}
Let ${\boldsymbol{p}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}'$ be a linearly independent family, let ${\mathrm{B}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ be
symmetric positive-definite with attainable null space ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$
(Definition~\ref{Def:AttainableNull}), and let ${\mathrm{A}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ be a continuous
operator that inverts ${\mathrm{B}}$ through the Hilbertian extensions, in the sense that
${\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{B}}=\mathrm{Id}$ on ${\mathcal{S}}$ and ${\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{A}}=\mathrm{Id}$ on ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$. Then, the
following statements are equivalent.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] \emph{(Attainability)} ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$.
\item[(ii)] \emph{(Invertibility through completion)} $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}})$ is a ${\boldsymbol{p}}$-conditional Riesz
pair (Definition~\ref{Def:Intrinsic}); equivalently, the Hilbertian extensions of $\pi{\mathrm{A}}$ and
${\mathrm{B}}$ are mutually inverse isometries between ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ and $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$.
\item[(iii)] \emph{(Minimality)} The operator ${\mathrm{A}}$ is ${\boldsymbol{p}}$-conditionally positive-definite and
its finiteness domain is the detrended space,
\begin{align*}
{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}={\mathcal{N}}^\perp_{\mathrm{B}}=\{\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}:\ \pi{\mathrm{A}}\varphi\in\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}\},
\end{align*}
which is the largest subspace of ${\mathcal{S}}$ on which the image ${\mathrm{A}}\varphi$ has finite energy. No
smaller conditioning is admissible: whenever ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\not\supseteq{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$, the space
${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}}}$ contains test functions whose image leaves $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$.
\end{enumerate}
When they hold, ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ is the trend space of the theory, and
$\langle{\mathrm{A}}\varphi,\varphi\rangle=\|\pi{\mathrm{A}}\varphi\|_{\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}^2=|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}^2$
for every $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
\emph{(i)$\Leftrightarrow$(ii)}:
Let $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$. By the Bridge below, ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}^\perp={\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, so that
$\pi{\mathrm{A}}\varphi\in\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ is represented by some $|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}}$-Cauchy sequence
$(\psi_k)\subset{\mathcal{S}}$ with $\pi\psi_k\to\pi({\mathrm{A}}\varphi)$. As ${\mathrm{A}}\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}'$, the relation
${\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{A}}=\mathrm{Id}$ on ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ then translates into ${\mathrm{B}}\psi_k\to\varphi$ in ${\mathcal{S}}'$, which completes the
sequential reading of Clause~(ii). For the isometry, the Cauchy--Schwarz bound
$|\langle{\mathrm{B}}\psi_k-{\mathrm{B}}\psi_j,\psi_k\rangle|\le|\psi_k-\psi_j|_{{\mathrm{B}}}|\psi_k|_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ gives
$\langle{\mathrm{B}}\psi_k,\psi_k\rangle-\langle\varphi,\psi_k\rangle\to0$.
Since $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, the weak-$\ast$ continuous functional $f\mapsto\langle \varphi,f\rangle$
vanishes on ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$, so that it descends to a continuous functional on
${\mathcal{S}}'/{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$. The convergence $\pi\psi_k\to\pi({\mathrm{A}}\varphi)$ therefore gives
$\langle\varphi,\psi_k\rangle\to\langle{\mathrm{A}}\varphi,\varphi\rangle$.
Hence
\begin{align*}
\|\pi{\mathrm{A}}\varphi\|_{\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}^2=\lim_{k\to\infty}|\psi_k|_{{\mathrm{B}}}^2
=\langle{\mathrm{A}}\varphi,\varphi\rangle=|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}^2 .
\end{align*}
The reverse composition follows from ${\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{B}}=\mathrm{Id}$ on ${\mathcal{S}}$, which extends to
$\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ by density of $\pi({\mathcal{S}})$. The converse is immediate, as Clause~(i) is part
of Definition~\ref{Def:Intrinsic}.


\medskip
\emph{Bridge}:
For $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}$, write
$\|\varphi\|_{{\mathrm{A}}}^\ast:=\sup_{\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}\setminus\{0\}}|\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle|/|\psi|_{{\mathrm{B}}}\in[0,+\infty]$
for the dual norm induced by $|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}}$. On ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, it coincides with the 
seminorm $|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}=\sqrt{\langle{\mathrm{A}}\varphi,\varphi\rangle}$ of
Definition~\ref{Def:CondPositiveDef2} (Proposition~\ref{Prop:Realization}), whereas on the remainder
of ${\mathcal{S}}$ the two need not agree and $\|\varphi\|_{{\mathrm{A}}}^\ast$ may be infinite. The image
${\mathrm{A}}\varphi$ (or rather $\pi{\mathrm{A}} \varphi$) lies in $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ if and only if the functional
$\psi\mapsto\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle$ is $|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}}$-bounded; that is,
$\|\varphi\|_{{\mathrm{A}}}^\ast<\infty$; and this holds if and only if $\varphi\in{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}^\perp$.
Indeed, if $\langle\varphi,p\rangle\neq0$ for some $p\in{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$, then
$p=\lim_k\chi_k$ with $|\chi_k|_{{\mathrm{B}}}\to0$, so that the quotient
$|\langle\varphi,\chi_k\rangle|/|\chi_k|_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ diverges; conversely, the inverse relation
${\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{B}}=\mathrm{Id}$ on ${\mathcal{S}}$ identifies $\|\varphi\|_{{\mathrm{A}}}^\ast$ with
$\|\pi{\mathrm{A}}\varphi\|_{\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}$, which is finite exactly on ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}^\perp$.
Therefore
\begin{align*}
\{\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}:\ \pi{\mathrm{A}}\varphi\in\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}\}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}^\perp .
\end{align*}

\medskip
\emph{(i)$\Rightarrow$(iii)}:
Let us assume that ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, so that
${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}^\perp$ is the finiteness domain identified in the Bridge.
For $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, the relation ${\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{A}}=\mathrm{Id}$ gives
\begin{align*}
\langle{\mathrm{A}}\varphi,\varphi\rangle
=\langle{\mathrm{A}}\varphi,{\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{A}}\varphi\rangle
=\|\pi{\mathrm{A}}\varphi\|_{\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}^2\ \ge\ 0 .
\end{align*}
This quantity vanishes only for $\varphi=0$: the identity
$\|\pi{\mathrm{A}}\varphi\|_{\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}=0$ means that ${\mathrm{A}}\varphi\in{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$, which the
extension of ${\mathrm{B}}$ annihilates (see the discussion that follows
Definition~\ref{Def:Intrinsic}), from which we get $\varphi={\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{A}}\varphi=0$. The operator
${\mathrm{A}}$ is therefore ${\boldsymbol{p}}$-conditionally positive-definite.

\medskip
\emph{(iii)$\Rightarrow$(i)}:
The Bridge identifies the finiteness domain of~(iii) as ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}^\perp$, so that the identity
of~(iii) reads ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}^\perp={\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}^\perp$. Let us apply $\perp$ once more: the
bipolar theorem gives $\overline{{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}^{\,w\ast}=\overline{{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}^{\,w\ast}$. The
space ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ is finite-dimensional, hence weak-$\ast$ closed, which yields
${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=\overline{{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}^{\,w\ast}\supseteq{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$. The attainable null
space ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ is then contained in a finite-dimensional space, so that it is itself
finite-dimensional and weak-$\ast$ closed. We conclude that ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, which
is~(i). Let us stress that the finite-dimensionality of ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ is not an assumption here:
it is a consequence of the finiteness-domain identity of~(iii).

\medskip
\emph{Minimality}:
Let us assume that ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\not\supseteq{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ and pick some
$p\in{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}\setminus{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}}}$. Since ${\mathcal{S}}$ separates the points of ${\mathcal{S}}'$ and
${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}}}$ is finite-dimensional, there is a test function
$\varphi\in{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}}}^\perp={\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}}}$ with $\langle\varphi,p\rangle\neq0$. Such a
$\varphi$ lies outside ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}^\perp$, which is precisely the region identified in the
Bridge: there, ${\mathrm{A}}\varphi$ leaves $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ and the dual norm
$\|\varphi\|_{{\mathrm{A}}}^\ast$ diverges. No conditioning smaller than ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ is
therefore admissible.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{Lem:Biorthogonal}
Let ${\boldsymbol{p}}=(p_1,\dots,p_{N_0})$ be a linearly independent family in ${\mathcal{S}}'$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item There is a family ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}=(\phi_1,\dots,\phi_{N_0})$ in ${\mathcal{S}}$ that is biorthogonal to ${\boldsymbol{p}}$, in
the sense that $\langle p_i,\phi_j\rangle=\delta_{ij}$ for all $i,j$.
\item For any such ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}$, we form the rank-$N_0$ operators
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}:=\sum_{n=1}^{N_0} p_n\,\langle p_n,\cdot\rangle:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}',
\qquad
{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}:=\sum_{n=1}^{N_0} \phi_n\,\langle \cdot,\phi_n\rangle:{\mathcal{S}}'\to{\mathcal{S}},
\end{align*}
which satisfy ${\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\varphi=0$ for every $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ and
${\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}={\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$. The operator
${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}:=\mathrm{Id}-{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ is a continuous projector of ${\mathcal{S}}$ onto ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$
obliquely along ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}:=\mathrm{span}({\boldsymbol{\phi}})$, so that ${\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$.
Its adjoint ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast=\mathrm{Id}-{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ is a projector of ${\mathcal{S}}'$ onto
${\mathcal{S}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}:=\{f\in{\mathcal{S}}':\langle f,\phi_k\rangle=0,\ k=1,\dots,N_0\}$ along ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
\emph{Existence}:
Let us fix $j\in\{1,\dots,N_0\}$ and set $M_j:=\bigcap_{i\neq j}\ker p_i\subset{\mathcal{S}}$. A continuous
linear functional on ${\mathcal{S}}$ belongs to the span of $\{p_i\}_{i\neq j}$ if and only if it vanishes on
$M_j$; this is the standard consequence of the Hahn--Banach theorem which identifies the annihilator of a
finite family of functionals \cite[Ch.~3]{Rudin1991}. Since ${\boldsymbol{p}}$ is linearly independent, $p_j$ is not in the span of
$(p_i)_{i\neq j}$, and therefore $p_j$ does not vanish on $M_j$. Consequently, there exists some $\psi_j\in M_j$ with
$\langle p_j,\psi_j\rangle\neq0$, and we set $\phi_j:=\psi_j/\langle p_j,\psi_j\rangle$. This gives
$\langle p_i,\phi_j\rangle=\delta_{ij}$ for all $i,j$, which is the announced biorthogonality.

\medskip
\emph{Projector relations}:
For $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}$, a direct computation gives
${\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\varphi=\sum_{k}\phi_k\langle p_k,\varphi\rangle$, because
$\langle p_n,\phi_k\rangle=\delta_{nk}$. If $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, then $\langle p_n,\varphi\rangle=0$
for all $n$, and hence ${\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\varphi=0$. Applying ${\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ to
${\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\varphi=\sum_k\phi_k\langle p_k,\varphi\rangle$ and using biorthogonality once
more yields ${\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\varphi=\sum_n p_n\langle p_n,\varphi\rangle={\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\varphi$,
which is the second stated relation.

\medskip
\emph{Projector}:
For every $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}$ and every $n$, we have
$\langle p_n,{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi\rangle=\langle p_n,\varphi\rangle-\sum_k\langle p_n,\phi_k\rangle\langle p_k,\varphi\rangle
=\langle p_n,\varphi\rangle-\langle p_n,\varphi\rangle=0$, and hence ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$. If
$\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, then ${\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\varphi=0$, and therefore ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi=\varphi$. It
follows that ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^2={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ and $\mathrm{ran}\,{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$. Moreover ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi=0$ holds if
and only if $\varphi={\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\varphi=\sum_k\phi_k\langle p_k,\varphi\rangle$, that is
$\varphi\in{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$; thus $\ker{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ and
${\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$.

\medskip
\emph{Adjoint}:
A direct computation gives $\langle{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast f,\varphi\rangle=\langle f,{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi\rangle$ for all
$f\in{\mathcal{S}}'$ and $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}$, which identifies ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast$ as the adjoint of ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$. The
relations $\ker{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, $\mathrm{ran}\,{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast={\mathcal{S}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$, and ${\mathcal{S}}'={\mathcal{S}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ follow by
transposition of those for ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{Thm:Concretization}
Let $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}})$ be a ${\boldsymbol{p}}$-conditional Riesz pair in the sense of
Definition~\ref{Def:Intrinsic}, so that the inversion identities \eqref{Eq:QuasiInverse} hold.
Let us fix a biorthogonal family ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}$ with
$\langle p_i,\phi_j\rangle=\delta_{ij}$, whose existence together with the projectors ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$
and ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast$ is granted by Lemma~\ref{Lem:Biorthogonal}, and let us set
${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}:={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast\,{\mathrm{A}}\,{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$. Then, the
following hold.
\begin{enumerate}
\item ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast$ restricts to an isometric identification
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast:\ \dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\
{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast\!\big(\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}\big)\subset{\mathcal{S}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}},
\end{align*}
which selects in each equivalence class the unique representative that annihilates ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}$. 
This enables us to identify a concrete dual of ${\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{A}}$ as ${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast(\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}})\cong \dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$.
\item The conditioned operator ${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ satisfies $\ker{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ and
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{B}}\,{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\ \text{on}\ {\mathcal{S}},
\qquad
{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\,{\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast\ \text{on}\ {\mathcal{S}}.
\end{align*}
\item The objects ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$, $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ and $\pi{\mathrm{A}}$ are independent of
${\boldsymbol{\phi}}$. 
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
\emph{Projectors and the identification}:
By Lemma~\ref{Lem:Biorthogonal}, ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ projects ${\mathcal{S}}$ onto ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ along
${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$, and ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast$ projects ${\mathcal{S}}'$ onto ${\mathcal{S}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ along ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$.
The hypothesis ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ shows that $\ker{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast={\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$, which means
that ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast$ annihilates exactly the null classes. It therefore descends to the stated isometry
from $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ onto its representatives in ${\mathcal{S}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$.

\medskip
\emph{Inversion relations}:
As noted after Definition~\ref{Def:Intrinsic}, the extension of ${\mathrm{B}}$ annihilates
${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}=\mathrm{span}({\boldsymbol{p}})$, from which we deduce that ${\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast={\mathrm{B}}$. The symmetry of ${\mathrm{B}}$ gives
$\langle p_k,{\mathrm{B}}\,\cdot\rangle=\langle{\mathrm{B}} p_k,\cdot\rangle=0$, which yields ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{B}}$.
Therefore
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast{\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}},
\qquad
{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\,{\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast{\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast{\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast.
\end{align*}
The last equality in the first chain applies the second identity of
\eqref{Eq:QuasiInverse} to ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, while the last equality in the
second chain applies the first identity of \eqref{Eq:QuasiInverse}. 

\medskip
\emph{Null space}:
Let us suppose that ${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi=0$. Then ${\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi\in\ker{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=\ker\pi$,
which gives $\pi{\mathrm{A}}({\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi)=0$. The map $\pi{\mathrm{A}}$ is injective on ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$, and
hence ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi=0$, that is $\varphi\in{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$. The reverse inclusion follows from
${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}={\boldsymbol{0}}$, which gives ${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}={\boldsymbol{0}}$. Therefore
$\ker{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$. Finally, for $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ we have
${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast{\mathrm{A}}\varphi$, which differs from ${\mathrm{A}}\varphi$ by an element of
${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=\ker\pi$; consequently, $\pi{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi=\pi{\mathrm{A}}\varphi$: the
conditioned operator represents the same isometry as ${\mathrm{A}}$.

\medskip
\emph{Independence}:
The spaces ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ and $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ and the map $\pi{\mathrm{A}}$ make no reference to
${\boldsymbol{\phi}}$.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{Thm:Augmentation}
Under the assumptions and notations of Theorem~\ref{Thm:Concretization}, and with the rank-$N_0$
operators ${\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ and ${\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ of Lemma~\ref{Lem:Biorthogonal}, we define the augmented
operators
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{A}}_+:={\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}+{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}},
\qquad
{\mathrm{B}}_+:={\mathrm{B}}+{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}.
\end{align*}
The notation ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}(f):=\big(\langle f,\phi_1\rangle,\dots,\langle f,\phi_{N_0}\rangle\big)$ collects the
${\boldsymbol{\phi}}$-coordinates of $f \in {\mathcal{S}}'$. Then, the following properties hold.
\begin{enumerate}
\item {\em Positivity:} The operators ${\mathrm{A}}_+$ and ${\mathrm{B}}_+$ are positive-definite on ${\mathcal{S}}$, with
\begin{align*}
|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}_+}^2=|{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}^2+|{\boldsymbol{p}}(\varphi)|_2^2,
\qquad
|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+}^2=|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{B}}}^2+|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\varphi)|_2^2,
\end{align*}
where $|\cdot|_2$ denotes the Euclidean norm on $ \mathbb{R}^{N_0}$.
\item {\em Inversion:} ${\mathrm{B}}_+{\mathrm{A}}_+=\mathrm{Id}$ and ${\mathrm{A}}_+{\mathrm{B}}_+=\mathrm{Id}$ on ${\mathcal{S}}$. In particular, no
conditioning and no projector remain.
\item {\em Direct sums:} The completions
\begin{align*}
{\mathcal{H}}:=\overline{({\mathcal{S}},|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{A}}_+})}
={\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}},
\qquad
{\mathcal{H}}':=\overline{({\mathcal{S}},|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+})}
={\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}
\end{align*}
are orthogonal direct sums, where ${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}:={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast(\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}})\subset{\mathcal{S}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$
is the concrete realization of $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$, and where the finite-dimensional blocks carry
the Euclidean metrics $|{\boldsymbol{p}}(\cdot)|_2$ and $|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\cdot)|_2$, respectively. Both spaces satisfy
${\mathcal{S}}\overset{d}{\hookrightarrow}{\mathcal{H}}\overset{d}{\hookrightarrow}{\mathcal{S}}'$ and ${\mathcal{S}}\overset{d}{\hookrightarrow}{\mathcal{H}}'\overset{d}{\hookrightarrow}{\mathcal{S}}'$ with the canonical
inclusions; they are Hilbert--Gelfand spaces.
\item {\em Duality:} The extension of the ${\mathcal{S}}$--${\mathcal{S}}'$ pairing identifies ${\mathcal{H}}'$ with the dual
of ${\mathcal{H}}$, and the pairing is block-diagonal with respect to the two direct sums. The induced
extensions
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{A}}_+:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}',
\qquad
{\mathrm{B}}_+:{\mathcal{H}}'\to{\mathcal{H}}
\end{align*}
are isometric, mutually inverse, and exchange the finite-dimensional blocks:
${\mathrm{A}}_+\phi_n=p_n$ and ${\mathrm{B}}_+p_n=\phi_n$ for $n=1,\dots,N_0$. Consequently, $({\mathrm{A}}_+,{\mathrm{B}}_+)$
is a compatible Riesz pair (the case $N_0=0$) for $({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}')$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
We first establish two key properties. The hypothesis ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ provides, for each $n$, a
sequence $(\chi^{(n)}_k)\subset{\mathcal{S}}$ such that $|\chi^{(n)}_k|_{{\mathrm{B}}}\to0$ and
$\chi^{(n)}_k\to p_n$ in ${\mathcal{S}}'$. The Cauchy--Schwarz inequality gives
$|\langle{\mathrm{B}}\varphi,\chi^{(n)}_k\rangle|\le|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{B}}}\,|\chi^{(n)}_k|_{{\mathrm{B}}}\to0$ for every
$\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}$. This controlled limit behavior has two consequences, which are central to the argument below:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)]  the extension
of ${\mathrm{B}}$ to ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ vanishes, so that ${\mathrm{B}} p_n=0$; and
\item[(ii)]  the pairing
$\langle p_n,{\mathrm{B}}\varphi\rangle:=\lim_k\langle\chi^{(n)}_k,{\mathrm{B}}\varphi\rangle=0$ is well defined
and independent of the chosen sequence.
\end{enumerate}

\medskip
\emph{Positivity}:
Every $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}$ decomposes as $\varphi={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi+{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^\mathsf{T}{\boldsymbol{p}}(\varphi)$. Since
${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast{\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$, we obtain
$\langle{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi,\varphi\rangle=\langle{\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi,{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi\rangle
=|{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}^2$ and
$\langle{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\varphi,\varphi\rangle=|{\boldsymbol{p}}(\varphi)|_2^2$, which proves the first norm identity.
Let us now suppose that $|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}_+}=0$. Then, ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi=0$ (because ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ and ${\mathrm{A}}$ is
${\boldsymbol{p}}$-conditionally positive-definite) and
${\boldsymbol{p}}(\varphi)={\boldsymbol{0}}$, which gives $\varphi=0$. The second identity follows from
$\langle{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi,\varphi\rangle=|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\varphi)|_2^2$, and the positivity of ${\mathrm{B}}_+$ is
immediate because $|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ is already a norm.

\medskip
\emph{Inversion}:
For $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}$, we expand the product into four terms:
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{B}}_+{\mathrm{A}}_+
={\mathrm{B}}\,{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}+{\mathrm{B}}\,{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}+{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\,{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}+{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\,{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}.
\end{align*}
Theorem~\ref{Thm:Concretization} gives ${\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$. Next,
${\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\varphi=\sum_n\langle p_n,\varphi\rangle\,{\mathrm{B}}p_n=0$ by property~(i).
Likewise, ${\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi=\sum_n\phi_n\langle{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi,\phi_n\rangle=0$,
because ${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$. Finally, the biorthogonality of $({\boldsymbol{p}}, {\boldsymbol{\phi}})$ yields
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\varphi
=\sum_{m,n}\phi_m\,\langle p_n,\varphi\rangle\,\langle p_n,\phi_m\rangle
=\sum_{n}\phi_n\,\langle p_n,\varphi\rangle
=(\mathrm{Id}-{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})\varphi .
\end{align*}
The sum of the four terms is ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}+(\mathrm{Id}-{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})=\mathrm{Id}$. For the reverse order, we expand
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{A}}_+{\mathrm{B}}_+
={\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\,{\mathrm{B}}+{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\,{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}+{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\,{\mathrm{B}}+{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\,{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}.
\end{align*}
Theorem~\ref{Thm:Concretization} gives ${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast$ on ${\mathcal{S}}$. Next,
${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi=0$, because
$\operatorname{ran}({\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}=\ker{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$. Property (ii) above gives
${\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}{\mathrm{B}}\varphi=\sum_n p_n\langle p_n,{\mathrm{B}}\varphi\rangle=0$. Finally,
${\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi=\sum_n p_n\langle \varphi,\phi_n\rangle=(\mathrm{Id}-{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast)\varphi$, and the sum is
${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast+(\mathrm{Id}-{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast)=\mathrm{Id}$. 

\medskip
\emph{Compatibility}:
We now verify Condition (ii) of Corollary~\ref{Cor:AlgebraicInverse}; i.e., that the
identities of Item 2 hold through the Hilbertian extensions of ${\mathrm{A}}_+$ and ${\mathrm{B}}_+$. This is equivalent to having the inclusions ${\mathrm{B}}_+({\mathcal{S}})\subset{\mathcal{H}}$ and
${\mathrm{A}}_+({\mathcal{S}})\subset{\mathcal{H}}'$, where ${\mathcal{H}}=\overline{({\mathcal{S}},|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{A}}_+})}$ and
${\mathcal{H}}'=\overline{({\mathcal{S}},|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+})}$. To prove the first inclusion, we take
${\mathrm{B}}_+\varphi={\mathrm{B}}\varphi+{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi$, where
${\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}$ and where ${\mathrm{B}}\varphi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ is the limit of an
$|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{A}}}$-Cauchy sequence in ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ (Definition~\ref{Def:Intrinsic}); by the
norm identity of Item 1, this sequence is also $|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{A}}_+}$-Cauchy, so that
${\mathrm{B}}_+\varphi\in{\mathcal{H}}$. For the second one, we write
${\mathrm{A}}_+\varphi={\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi+{\mathrm{R}}_{\boldsymbol{p}} \varphi$ and pick a
$|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}}$-Cauchy sequence $(\psi_k)\subset{\mathcal{S}}$ that represents the class
$\pi{\mathrm{A}}({\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi)$, so that ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast\psi_k\to{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi$ in ${\mathcal{S}}'$, the difficulty here being that ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast\psi_k \notin {\mathcal{S}}$. Hence our strategy is to
correct the ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}$-coordinates of the $\psi_k$ with the sequences of the preamble: for each $k$, we set
$\tilde\psi_k:=\psi_k+\sum_n\big({\boldsymbol{p}}(\varphi)_n-{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\psi_k)_n\big)\chi^{(n)}_{j(k)}
\in{\mathcal{S}}$, where the index $j(k)$ is so large that the correction perturbs the
$|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}}$-seminorm, the ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}$-coordinates, and the convergence in ${\mathcal{S}}'$ by at
most $1/k$. By the norm identity of Item 1, the sequence $(\tilde\psi_k)$ is
$|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+}$-Cauchy with $\tilde\psi_k\to{\mathrm{A}}_+\varphi$ in ${\mathcal{S}}'$; hence
${\mathrm{A}}_+\varphi\in{\mathcal{H}}'$. Corollary~\ref{Cor:AlgebraicInverse} then shows that
$({\mathrm{A}}_+,{\mathrm{B}}_+)$ is a compatible Riesz pair, and Theorem~\ref{Theo:HilbertPositiveDef}
realizes ${\mathcal{H}}$ and ${\mathcal{H}}'$ as mutually dual Hilbert--Gelfand spaces whose pairing extends
the canonical one and whose Riesz maps are the mutually inverse isometric extensions of
${\mathrm{A}}_+$ and ${\mathrm{B}}_+$. This proves Items 3 and 4, up to the identification of the blocks.

\medskip
\emph{Block structure}:
Let $u\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ and ${\bf{c}}\in \mathbb{R}^{N_0}$. Then
$\langle{\mathrm{A}}_+u,{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^\mathsf{T}{\bf{c}}\rangle
=\langle{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}u,{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^\mathsf{T}{\bf{c}}\rangle+{\boldsymbol{p}}(u)^\mathsf{T}{\bf{c}}=0$, because
${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}u\in{\mathcal{S}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ and ${\boldsymbol{p}}(u)={\boldsymbol{0}}$; likewise,
$\langle{\mathrm{A}}_+({\boldsymbol{\phi}}^\mathsf{T}{\bf{c}}),u\rangle=\langle{\boldsymbol{p}}^\mathsf{T}{\bf{c}},u\rangle=0$. The norm identity of
Item 1 is therefore an orthogonal decomposition of ${\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$.
Since ${\mathcal{H}}$ is realized in ${\mathcal{S}}'$, the two blocks of any $|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{A}}_+}$-Cauchy
sequence converge separately in ${\mathcal{S}}'$---the first in ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$, and the second in the
finite-dimensional, hence closed, space ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$---which gives
${\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$.

On the dual side, the preamble sequences give ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\subset{\mathcal{H}}'$: the sequence
$(\chi^{(n)}_k)$ is $|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+}$-Cauchy, because $|\chi^{(n)}_k|_{{\mathrm{B}}}\to0$ and
${\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\chi^{(n)}_k)\to{\boldsymbol{e}}_n$, and it converges to $p_n$ in ${\mathcal{S}}'$, with
$\|p_n\|_{{\mathcal{H}}'}=1$. Likewise, for $g\in{\mathcal{S}}$, the corrected sequence
$g-\sum_n{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(g)_n\,\chi^{(n)}_k$ converges to ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast g$ with
$\|{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast g\|_{{\mathcal{H}}'}=|g|_{{\mathrm{B}}}$; since ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast({\mathcal{S}})$ is dense in
${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast(\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}})$ and ${\mathcal{H}}'$ is complete, we obtain
${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}\subset{\mathcal{H}}'$. Conversely, the seminorm $|f|_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ depends only on the
class $\pi f$, so that the decomposition $f={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast f+{\boldsymbol{p}}^\mathsf{T}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(f)$ of
Lemma~\ref{Lem:Biorthogonal} gives
$\|f\|_{{\mathcal{H}}'}^2=\|{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast f\|_{\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}^2+|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(f)|_2^2$ for every
$f\in{\mathcal{S}}$, which extends by density and identifies ${\mathcal{H}}'$ with the orthogonal direct sum
${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$.

\medskip
\emph{Block-diagonal duality and exchange of the blocks}:
Let $h=u+{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^\mathsf{T}{\bf{c}}\in{\mathcal{H}}$ and $f=v+{\boldsymbol{p}}^\mathsf{T}{\bf{d}}\in{\mathcal{H}}'$ with
$(u,v)\in{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}\times{\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ and ${\bf{c}},{\bf{d}}\in \mathbb{R}^{N_0}$. The extended pairing
splits into four terms: $\langle v,u\rangle$ is the
$\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$--${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ duality of the conditional pair;
$\langle v,{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^\mathsf{T}{\bf{c}}\rangle=0$, because $v$ annihilates ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}$;
$\langle{\boldsymbol{p}}^\mathsf{T}{\bf{d}},u\rangle=0$, because $u$ annihilates ${\boldsymbol{p}}$; and
$\langle{\boldsymbol{p}}^\mathsf{T}{\bf{d}},{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^\mathsf{T}{\bf{c}}\rangle={\bf{d}}^\mathsf{T}{\bf{c}}$, by biorthogonality. The pairing is
therefore block-diagonal. Finally, since ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\phi_n=0$ and by biorthogonality,
${\mathrm{A}}_+\phi_n={\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\phi_n+{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\phi_n=p_n$, while property (i) of the
preamble gives ${\mathrm{B}}_+p_n={\mathrm{B}}p_n+{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}p_n=\phi_n$; on the primary block,
${\mathrm{A}}_+u={\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}u$ is the intrinsic isometry $\pi{\mathrm{A}}$ in its concrete realization
(Theorem~\ref{Thm:Concretization}). This completes the identification of the Riesz maps and
the proof.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{Cor:PhiIndependence}
Under the assumptions of Theorem~\ref{Thm:Augmentation}, let ${\boldsymbol{\phi}},\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}$ be two families that are biorthogonal to ${\boldsymbol{p}}$, and let $({\mathcal{H}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}},{\mathcal{H}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})$ and $({\mathcal{H}}_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}},{\mathcal{H}}'_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}})$ denote the corresponding augmented pairs, with norms $|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{A}}_+},|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+}$ and $|\cdot|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{A}}}_+},|\cdot|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{B}}}_+}$, respectively. Then, the following properties hold.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Canonical description}: As subsets of ${\mathcal{S}}'$,
\begin{align}
\label{Eq:CanonicalHprime}
{\mathcal{H}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}=\pi^{-1}\big(\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}\big)=\{f\in{\mathcal{S}}':\ \pi f\in\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}\},
\qquad
{\mathcal{H}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}+{\mathcal{S}},
\end{align}
where $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ is realized in ${\mathcal{S}}'/{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ (Proposition~\ref{Prop:Realization}, Item 3). In particular, ${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathcal{H}}'_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}}$ and ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathcal{H}}_{\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}}$ as sets.
\item \emph{Equivalence of norms}: The norms $|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+}$ and $|\cdot|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{B}}}_+}$ are equivalent on ${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$, and the norms $|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{A}}_+}$ and $|\cdot|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{A}}}_+}$ are equivalent on ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$.
\item \emph{Quotient structure}: For every ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}$, the quotient map $\pi$ induces an isometric isomorphism from ${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}/{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, equipped with the quotient norm $\inf_{q\in{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}|f+q|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+}$, onto $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$. The orthogonal complement of ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ in ${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ is ${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}={\mathcal{S}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$; it is the only object that depends on ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
\emph{Canonical description}:
Let $f={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast g+q\in{\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ with $g\in\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$. Since ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast$ selects a representative of the class $g$ and since $\pi q=0$, we have that $\pi f=g\in\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$. Conversely, let $f\in{\mathcal{S}}'$ be such that $\pi f\in\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$. Lemma~\ref{Lem:Biorthogonal} gives $f={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast f+{\boldsymbol{p}}^\mathsf{T}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(f)$, where ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast f$ is the representative of the class $\pi f$ that annihilates ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}$, so that ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast f\in{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast(\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}})={\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}$. Hence $f\in{\mathcal{H}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$, which proves the first identity. For the second one, the splitting ${\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ of Lemma~\ref{Lem:Biorthogonal} and the inclusion ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\subset{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ give ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}+{\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}+{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathcal{H}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$. Neither description refers to ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}$.

\medskip
\emph{Equivalence of norms}:
Item 3 of Theorem~\ref{Thm:Augmentation} gives $|f|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{B}}}_+}^2=\|\pi f\|_{\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}^2+|\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}(f)|_2^2$ for every $f\in{\mathcal{H}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$. Since $\tilde\phi_n\in{\mathcal{S}}$, the functional $f\mapsto\langle f,\tilde\phi_n\rangle$ is continuous on ${\mathcal{S}}'$ and, therefore, on ${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$, which is continuously embedded in ${\mathcal{S}}'$. Consequently, there is a constant $C$ such that $|\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}(f)|_2\le C|f|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+}$, which yields $|f|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{B}}}_+}\le\sqrt{1+C^2}\,|f|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+}$. The reverse inequality follows by exchanging the roles of ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}$ and $\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$. On the dual side, let $h=u+{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^\mathsf{T}{\bf{c}}=\tilde u+\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\mathsf{T}\tilde{{\bf{c}}}$ be the two decompositions of $h\in{\mathcal{H}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$, with $u,\tilde u\in{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ and ${\bf{c}},\tilde{{\bf{c}}}\in \mathbb{R}^{N_0}$, so that $|h|_{{\mathrm{A}}_+}^2=\|u\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}}^2+|{\bf{c}}|_2^2$ and $|h|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{A}}}_+}^2=\|\tilde u\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}}^2+|\tilde{{\bf{c}}}|_2^2$. The test function ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}^\mathsf{T}{\bf{c}}-\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\mathsf{T}\tilde{{\bf{c}}}=\tilde u-u$ lies in ${\mathcal{S}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}={\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ (a test function $\varphi\in{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ satisfies $|\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle|\le\|\varphi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}}|\psi|_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ by Proposition~\ref{Prop:Realization}, so that $\varphi\in{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}^\perp={\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ by the Bridge in the proof of Theorem~\ref{Thm:AttainableNull}), so that the application of ${\boldsymbol{p}}$ gives $\tilde{{\bf{c}}}={\bf{c}}$. Hence $\tilde u=u+\sum_n c_n(\phi_n-\tilde\phi_n)$ with $\phi_n-\tilde\phi_n\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\subset{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$, and the triangle inequality yields $|h|_{\tilde{{\mathrm{A}}}_+}^2\le\big(2+2\sum_n\|\phi_n-\tilde\phi_n\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}}^2\big)|h|_{{\mathrm{A}}_+}^2$. The reverse inequality follows by symmetry.

\medskip
\emph{Quotient structure}:
Since the direct sum ${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ is orthogonal, the distance from $f$ to ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ is the norm of its component in ${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}$, namely $\|{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast f\|_{{\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}}=\|\pi f\|_{\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}$ by Item 1 of Theorem~\ref{Thm:Concretization}. The map $\pi$ is onto $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ by \eqref{Eq:CanonicalHprime} and has null space ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, which proves the isometry. The identification of the orthogonal complement is Item 3 of Theorem~\ref{Thm:Augmentation} together with ${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{Rem:RigidityAugmented}
Corollary~\ref{Cor:PhiIndependence} complements the rigidity statement at the end of Section~\ref{Sec:AbstractFormulation}: the conditioning ${\boldsymbol{p}}$ is dictated by ${\mathrm{B}}$, and the resulting Hilbert--Gelfand spaces ${\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}+{\mathcal{S}}$ and ${\mathcal{H}}'=\pi^{-1}(\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}})$ are dictated by $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}})$ up to an equivalence of norms. The norm itself is not determined by $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}})$, nor is it determined by the sets: every continuous symmetric positive-semi-definite operator ${\mathrm{C}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ whose quadratic form is bounded on ${\mathcal{H}}'$ and positive-definite on ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ yields, through ${\mathrm{B}}+{\mathrm{C}}$, the same set ${\mathcal{H}}'$ with an equivalent norm but with another Riesz pair. What singles out the augmentations ${\mathrm{B}}_+={\mathrm{B}}+{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ among these is Item 3 of the corollary: the quotient map $\pi$ is a partial isometry, in the sense that $|f|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+}=\|\pi f\|_{\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}$ on the orthogonal complement of ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$. This property is investigated further in Proposition~\ref{Prop:NativeUniqueness}, in the operator-based setting of Section~\ref{Sec:OperatorConstructions}. Let us also note that Corollary~\ref{Cor:PhiIndependence} extends verbatim to biorthogonal families $\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ taken in the dual ${\mathcal{H}}$ rather than in ${\mathcal{S}}$, since its proof only uses the continuity of $f\mapsto\langle f,\tilde\phi_n\rangle$ on ${\mathcal{H}}'$.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
\label{Ex:LinearSpline}
Let $({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}')=({\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R}),{\mathcal{S}}'( \mathbb{R}))$ and ${\mathrm{D}}=\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d} x}$, so that
${\mathrm{D}}^\ast=-{\mathrm{D}}$ and
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{D}}^\ast{\mathrm{D}}=-{\mathrm{D}}^2,\qquad \widehat{{\mathrm{B}}\varphi}(\omega)=\omega^2\,\hat\varphi(\omega),
\end{align*}
where $\hat\varphi(\omega)=\int_ \mathbb{R}\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega x}\,{\rm d} x$. The operator ${\mathrm{B}}$ is
symmetric and positive-definite on ${\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R})$, because
$\langle{\mathrm{B}}\varphi,\varphi\rangle=\|{\mathrm{D}}\varphi\|_{L_2}^2>0$ for $\varphi\neq0$. Its
distributional null space $\ker({\mathrm{B}}:{\mathcal{S}}'\to{\mathcal{S}}')=\{a+bx:a,b\in \mathbb{R}\}$ is the space of affine
functions, but only the constants are attainable in the energy: the relevant null space is
${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=\mathrm{span}(p_1)$, with
$p_1\equiv1$ and $N_0=1$. Since
$\langle p_1,\varphi\rangle=\int_ \mathbb{R}\varphi(x){\rm d} x=\hat\varphi(0)$,
\begin{align*}
{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=\Big\{\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R}):\ \int_ \mathbb{R}\varphi(x){\rm d} x=0\Big\}.
\end{align*}

\medskip
\emph{Biorthogonal function and projector}:
Among the infinitely many admissible choices of $\phi_1\in{\mathcal{S}}$, we select the normalized Gaussian
\begin{align*}
\phi_1(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-x^2/2}\in{\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R}),
\qquad \langle p_1,\phi_1\rangle=\int_ \mathbb{R}\phi_1(x)\,{\rm d} x=1.
\end{align*}
The corresponding
projector of ${\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R})$ onto ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ along
${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}=\mathrm{span}(\phi_1)$ is
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}=\mathrm{Id}-\phi_1\,\langle p_1,\cdot\rangle:\ \varphi\mapsto\varphi-\phi_1\int_ \mathbb{R}\varphi(x)\,{\rm d} x,
\end{align*}
with adjoint ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast=\mathrm{Id}-p_1\,\langle\cdot,\phi_1\rangle$. The construction below does not
depend on this particular choice.

\medskip
\emph{Conditional inverse}:
Let ${\mathrm{A}}:\varphi\mapsto g\ast\varphi$ with $g(x)=-\tfrac12|x|={\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\{\frac{1}{\omega^2}\}(x)$ be the symmetric convolution inverse of
${\mathrm{B}}$ that solves ${\mathrm{B}}g=\delta$; it is such that ${\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{A}}=\mathrm{Id}$
on ${\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R})$. The fundamental solution $g$ is determined only up to an affine function,
which is why we replace ${\mathrm{A}}$ by the conditioned operator
${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast\,{\mathrm{A}}\,{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$. The latter is insensitive to
that ambiguity and satisfies
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{B}}\,{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}},\qquad {\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\,{\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast,\qquad
\ker{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}=\mathrm{span}(\phi_1).
\end{align*}

\medskip
\emph{Conditional positivity}:
Since  ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi=\varphi$ for $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, the induced quadratic form is that of ${\mathrm{A}}$:
\begin{align*}
\langle{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi,\varphi\rangle
=\langle{\mathrm{A}}\varphi,\varphi\rangle
=-\tfrac12\iint_{ \mathbb{R}^2}|x-y|\,\varphi(x)\varphi(y)\,{\rm d} x\,{\rm d} y
=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_ \mathbb{R}\frac{|\hat\varphi(\omega)|^2}{\omega^2}\,{\rm d}\omega,
\end{align*}
where the last (Fourier) expression is finite and strictly positive on ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$. The spatial
form applies to every $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R})$ and is strictly negative whenever $\varphi\ge0$ carries
a nonzero mass. Hence ${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ and ${\mathrm{A}}$ are ${\boldsymbol{p}}$-conditionally positive-definite but
not positive-definite, whereas ${\mathrm{B}}$ is positive-definite.

\medskip
\emph{Hilbert--Gelfand spaces and Riesz isometry}:
With $|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}^2=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\int_ \mathbb{R}\frac{|\hat\varphi(\omega)|^2}{\omega^2}\,{\rm d}\omega$ and
$|f|_{{\mathrm{B}}}^2=\|f'\|_{L_2}^2$,
\begin{align*}
{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}=\overline{({\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}},|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{A}}})}=\dot H^{-1}( \mathbb{R})\subset{\mathcal{S}}'( \mathbb{R}),
\qquad
\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}=\overline{({\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R}),|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}})}=\dot H^{1}( \mathbb{R}).
\end{align*}
The space ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ can be identified as the space of distributions with mean zero and Sobolev regularity $-1$. The space $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}=\dot H^1( \mathbb{R})$ is a space of
functions modulo constants: the energy $\|f'\|_{L_2}$ vanishes on ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\cong \mathbb{R}$, so that
$\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ is realized in the quotient ${\mathcal{S}}'( \mathbb{R})/ \mathbb{R}$
rather
than as a subspace of ${\mathcal{S}}'( \mathbb{R})$. The two spaces are dual under the ${\mathcal{S}}$--${\mathcal{S}}'$ pairing,
which is well defined because the mean-zero constraint on ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ makes it insensitive to
the added constant. The Riesz maps ${\mathrm{A}}:\dot H^{-1}( \mathbb{R})\to\dot H^{1}( \mathbb{R})$ and
${\mathrm{B}}:\dot H^{1}( \mathbb{R})\to\dot H^{-1}( \mathbb{R})$ are isometric and mutually inverse, since, for
$\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$,
\begin{align*}
|{\mathrm{A}}\varphi|_{{\mathrm{B}}}^2=\langle{\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{A}}\varphi,{\mathrm{A}}\varphi\rangle=\langle{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi,{\mathrm{A}}\varphi\rangle=\langle\varphi,{\mathrm{A}}\varphi\rangle=|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}^2 .
\end{align*}
Thus $g(x-y)=-\tfrac12|x-y|$ is the conditionally positive-definite kernel of the first-order spline
energy $\|f'\|_{L_2}^2$, and $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}})$ is a ${\boldsymbol{p}}$-conditional Riesz pair in the sense of
Definition~\ref{Def:Intrinsic}.

\medskip
\emph{Augmentation}:
Theorem~\ref{Thm:Augmentation} produces the unconditioned pair
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{A}}_+={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast{\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}+p_1\langle p_1,\cdot\rangle,
\qquad
{\mathrm{B}}_+=-{\mathrm{D}}^2+\phi_1\langle\cdot,\phi_1\rangle,
\end{align*}
with norms $|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}_+}^2=|{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}^2+\big(\int_ \mathbb{R}\varphi(x)\,{\rm d} x\big)^2$ and
$|f|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+}^2=\|f'\|_{L_2}^2+\langle f,\phi_1\rangle^2$. The induced dual pair of
Hilbert--Gelfand spaces is
\begin{align*}
{\mathcal{H}}&=\dot H^{-1}( \mathbb{R})\oplus\mathrm{span}(\phi_1),\\
{\mathcal{H}}'&={\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{D}}=\{f\in{\mathcal{S}}'( \mathbb{R}):f'\in L_2( \mathbb{R})\}={\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}\oplus\mathrm{span}(1),
\end{align*}
and the exchange of the finite-dimensional blocks reads ${\mathrm{A}}_+\phi_1=p_1=1$ and ${\mathrm{B}}_+1=\phi_1$.
The resulting ``native'' space ${\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{D}}=\overline{({\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R}),|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{D}}^\ast{\mathrm{D}} +{\mathrm{R}}_{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})}$ is strictly larger than the classical Sobolev space $H^1( \mathbb{R})$: it contains the constants and, more generally, every $f$ with $f'\in L_2( \mathbb{R})$, whatever its growth (for instance, $\ln(1+|x|)$). The classical norm $\|f'\|_{L_2}^2+|f(0)|^2$ of this space is of the augmented form with $\tilde\phi_1=\delta$, which is not a test function but a continuous functional on ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{D}}}$; Proposition~\ref{Prop:NativeUniqueness} below explains in which sense the two norms define the same native space.
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{Cor:GreenNormal}
Let ${\boldsymbol{p}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}'$ be a linearly independent family, let ${\mathrm{L}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to L_2$ be a
continuous and injective linear operator, and let us assume
that the normal operator ${\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{L}}^\ast{\mathrm{L}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ admits a
Green
inverse ${\mathrm{A}}=({\mathrm{L}}^\ast{\mathrm{L}})^{-1}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ through the Hilbertian extensions, in
the sense that ${\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{B}}=\mathrm{Id}$ on ${\mathcal{S}}$ and ${\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{A}}=\mathrm{Id}$ on
${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$. Then, the following statements are equivalent.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}})$ is a ${\boldsymbol{p}}$-conditional Riesz pair for $({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}')$.
\item[(ii)] The family ${\boldsymbol{p}}$ spans the attainable null space of the spline energy:
${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$.
\item[(iii)] The detrended space is the finiteness domain of the dual energy:
\begin{align*}
{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}
=\Big\{\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}:\ \sup_{\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}\backslash\{0\}}
\frac{|\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle|}{\|{\mathrm{L}}\psi\|_{L_2}}<\infty\Big\}.
\end{align*}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
The operator ${\mathrm{B}}$ is symmetric and positive-definite with
$|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{B}}}=\|{\mathrm{L}}\varphi\|_{L_2}$, which is a genuine norm because ${\mathrm{L}}$ is
injective. The equivalence of (i) and (ii) is then Theorem~\ref{Thm:AttainableNull}. For the
equivalence with (iii), the Bridge in the proof of that theorem identifies the finiteness
domain of the dual energy with ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}^\perp$, so that (iii) reads
${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}^\perp={\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}^\perp$; the bipolar argument of Step
(iii)$\Rightarrow$(i) of the same proof then converts this identity into (ii).
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{Lem:UnconditionedCriterion}
Let $({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}')$ be a nuclear Gelfand pair, ${\mathcal{K}}$ be a Hilbert space, and 
${\mathrm{T}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{K}}$ be a continuous and injective linear operator, with
${\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathrm{T}}}:=\overline{{\mathrm{T}}({\mathcal{S}})}^{\,{\mathcal{K}}}$. Then, there exists a continuous
operator ${\mathrm{A}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ such that $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{T}}^\ast{\mathrm{T}})$ is a compatible Riesz
pair for $({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}')$ if and only if the two following conditions hold.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] \emph{(Representation)} For every $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}$, there exists
$e_\varphi\in{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathrm{T}}}$ such that ${\mathrm{T}}^\ast e_\varphi=\iota\varphi$.
\item[(b)] \emph{(Sharpness)} The subspace ${\mathcal{E}}:=\{e_\varphi:\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}\}$ is
dense in ${\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathrm{T}}}$.
\end{enumerate}
When they hold, the form of the (unique) admissible operator is
$\langle{\mathrm{A}}\varphi,\psi\rangle=\langle e_\varphi,e_\psi\rangle_{{\mathcal{K}}}$, so that
$|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}=\|e_\varphi\|_{{\mathcal{K}}}$, and the extension of ${\mathrm{T}}$ is an isometric
isomorphism $\widetilde{{\mathrm{T}}}:{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{T}}^\ast{\mathrm{T}}}\to{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathrm{T}}}$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
We write ${\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{T}}^\ast{\mathrm{T}}$, so that $|\psi|_{{\mathrm{B}}}=\|{\mathrm{T}}\psi\|_{{\mathcal{K}}}$ is a norm on
${\mathcal{S}}$ by the injectivity of ${\mathrm{T}}$, and we introduce the dual energy
\begin{align*}
Q(\varphi):=\sup_{\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}\backslash\{0\}}
\frac{|\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle|^2}{|\psi|_{{\mathrm{B}}}^2}\in[0,+\infty].
\end{align*}

\medskip
\emph{Representers}:
The identity $\langle{\mathrm{T}}^\ast w,\psi\rangle=\langle w,{\mathrm{T}}\psi\rangle_{{\mathcal{K}}}$ gives
$\ker({\mathrm{T}}^\ast)={\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathrm{T}}}^\perp$, so that ${\mathrm{T}}^\ast$ is injective on
${\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathrm{T}}}$. For $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}$ fixed, we then consider the linear functional 
\begin{align*}
\ell_\varphi:{\mathrm{T}}({\mathcal{S}})\to \mathbb{R},
\qquad
\ell_\varphi({\mathrm{T}}\psi):=\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle ,
\end{align*}
which is well defined because ${\mathrm{T}}$ is injective. Its norm on
${\mathrm{T}}({\mathcal{S}})\subseteq{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathrm{T}}}$ is
\begin{align*}
\|\ell_\varphi\|
=\sup_{\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}\backslash\{0\}}
\frac{|\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle|}{\|{\mathrm{T}}\psi\|_{{\mathcal{K}}}}
=\sqrt{Q(\varphi)} ,
\end{align*}
so that $\ell_\varphi$ is continuous exactly when $Q(\varphi)<\infty$. In that case,
$\ell_\varphi$ extends uniquely  with the same norm to ${\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathrm{T}}}$. Since ${\mathcal{R}}_{\mathrm{T}}$ is a closed subspace
of ${\mathcal{K}}$, it is itself a Hilbert space. The Riesz representation theorem on
${\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathrm{T}}}$ then provides a unique $e_\varphi\in{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathrm{T}}}$ such that
\begin{align*}
\ell_\varphi(v)=\langle e_\varphi,v\rangle_{{\mathcal{K}}}
\quad\text{for all }v\in{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathrm{T}}},
\qquad
\|e_\varphi\|_{{\mathcal{K}}}=\|\ell_\varphi\|=\sqrt{Q(\varphi)} .
\end{align*}
The choice $v={\mathrm{T}}\psi$ gives
$\langle e_\varphi,{\mathrm{T}}\psi\rangle_{{\mathcal{K}}}=\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle$ for every
$\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$, which is the identity ${\mathrm{T}}^\ast e_\varphi=\iota\varphi$. Conversely, the
Cauchy--Schwarz inequality applied to that identity gives
$Q(\varphi)\le\|e_\varphi\|^2_{{\mathcal{K}}}<\infty$. We have therefore established the pointwise
criterion
\begin{align}
\label{Eq:RepresenterCriterion}
Q(\varphi)<\infty
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\exists\,e_\varphi\in{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathrm{T}}}:\ {\mathrm{T}}^\ast e_\varphi=\iota\varphi ,
\end{align}
in which case $e_\varphi$ is unique, $Q(\varphi)=\|e_\varphi\|^2_{{\mathcal{K}}}$, and
\begin{align}
\label{Eq:Reproducing}
\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle=\langle e_\varphi,{\mathrm{T}}\psi\rangle_{{\mathcal{K}}},
\qquad\psi\in{\mathcal{S}},
\end{align}
which we refer to as the reproducing identity. Equivalently,
\begin{align*}
e_\varphi=\big({\mathrm{T}}^\ast|_{{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathrm{T}}}}\big)^{-1}\iota\varphi ,
\end{align*}
where the restriction to ${\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathrm{T}}}$ is what makes ${\mathrm{T}}^\ast$ invertible. The criterion
\eqref{Eq:RepresenterCriterion} trades the computation of a supremum for the resolution of the
equation ${\mathrm{T}}^\ast e=\iota\varphi$ in ${\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathrm{T}}}$, the divergent case included: no
solution means $Q(\varphi)=\infty$. Condition (a) requires
\eqref{Eq:RepresenterCriterion} to hold for every $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}$.
The target ${\mathcal{K}}$ enters only through ${\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathrm{T}}}$, which is why no density assumption
on ${\mathrm{T}}({\mathcal{S}})$ is needed.

\medskip
\emph{Continuity}:
Let us assume (a). The family $\{|\psi|_{{\mathrm{B}}}^{-1}\iota\psi\}_{\psi\neq0}$ is pointwise
bounded on ${\mathcal{S}}$, which is barrelled, so that the Banach--Steinhaus theorem for barrelled
spaces \cite[Ch.~III]{Schaefer-Wolff1999} makes $\varphi\mapsto\sqrt{Q(\varphi)}$ a continuous
seminorm. The Gram form
$(\varphi,\psi)\mapsto\langle e_\varphi,e_\psi\rangle_{{\mathcal{K}}}$ therefore specifies a
continuous operator ${\mathrm{A}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'$, which is positive-definite because
${\mathrm{T}}^\ast e_\varphi=\iota\varphi\neq0$ for $\varphi\neq0$.

\medskip
\emph{The two dual norms}:
The identity $|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}=\|e_\varphi\|_{{\mathcal{K}}}=\sqrt{Q(\varphi)}$ is the second
identity of Corollary~\ref{Cor:AlgebraicInverse}(iii). For the first one, the reproducing
identity converts the supremum into the norm of an orthogonal projection,
\begin{align*}
\sup_{\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}\backslash\{0\}}
\frac{|\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle|}{|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}}
=\sup_{\varphi\neq0}
\frac{|\langle e_\varphi,{\mathrm{T}}\psi\rangle_{{\mathcal{K}}}|}{\|e_\varphi\|_{{\mathcal{K}}}}
=\big\|{\mathrm{Q}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}\,{\mathrm{T}}\psi\big\|_{{\mathcal{K}}},
\end{align*}
where ${\mathrm{Q}}_{{\mathcal{E}}}$ denotes the orthogonal projector onto $\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}$. This quantity
equals $|\psi|_{{\mathrm{B}}}=\|{\mathrm{T}}\psi\|_{{\mathcal{K}}}$ for every $\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$ if and only if
${\mathrm{T}}({\mathcal{S}})\subseteq\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}$; that is, if and only if (b) holds.

\medskip
\emph{Conclusion}:
Under (a) and (b), Corollary~\ref{Cor:AlgebraicInverse}(iii) is met, so that $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}})$
is a compatible Riesz pair, with ${\mathrm{A}}$ unique and symmetric by
Remark~\ref{Rem:AutomaticSymmetry}. Conversely, the same corollary applied to such a pair
supplies the two identities: the second gives $Q<\infty$ on ${\mathcal{S}}$, hence (a), and the first
gives ${\mathrm{T}}({\mathcal{S}})\subseteq\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}$, hence (b). Finally, $\widetilde{{\mathrm{T}}}$ is the isometric extension of $\psi\mapsto{\mathrm{T}}\psi$ from the
dense subspace ${\mathcal{S}}$ of ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$, whose range is ${\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathrm{T}}}$.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{Rem:SchwartzHilbertian}
Lemma~\ref{Lem:UnconditionedCriterion} is a reformulation, in terms of the energy rather than
of the kernel, of the classical correspondence between the Hilbertian subspaces of a locally
