convex space and the positive kernels that they induce \cite{Schwartz1964}. The same comment
applies to Theorem~\ref{Theo:HilbertPositiveDef}, which is the version of that correspondence
that is adapted to a nuclear Gelfand pair. The realization
${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}={\mathrm{T}}^\ast({\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathrm{T}}})$ with the transported norm is the operator-range
construction of \cite{Fillmore-Williams1971}, while the reproducing-kernel counterpart is due
to Aronszajn \cite{Aronszajn1950}. What is specific to our formulation is that the two
conditions are stated on $\|{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\|_{{\mathcal{K}}}$, which is the regularization energy of the
variational problem, and that they survive the conditioning. The classical correspondence, by
contrast, fails precisely when the energy is only a seminorm, which is the situation of the
Beppo--Levi spaces \cite{Deny-Lions1954}. The augmentation ${\mathrm{B}}_+=\Psi^\ast\Psi$ in the
proof of Theorem~\ref{Thm:NormalPair} is what restores its applicability.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{Thm:NormalPair}
Let ${\mathrm{L}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to L_2$ be a continuous and injective linear operator with adjoint
${\mathrm{L}}^\ast:L_2\to{\mathcal{S}}'$, and set
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{L}}^\ast{\mathrm{L}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}',
\qquad
{\mathcal{R}}=\overline{{\mathrm{L}}({\mathcal{S}})}^{\,L_2},
\end{align*}
so that ${\mathrm{B}}$ is symmetric and positive-definite with
$|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{B}}}=\|{\mathrm{L}}\varphi\|_{L_2}$, and
$\ker({\mathrm{L}}^\ast)={\mathcal{R}}^{\perp}$. Then, there exists a continuous operator
${\mathrm{A}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ such that $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}})$ is a ${\boldsymbol{p}}$-conditional Riesz pair for
$({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}')$ (Definition~\ref{Def:Intrinsic}) if and only if the following three
conditions hold.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Attainable annihilation of ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$}:
 for every $n\in\{1,\dots,N_0\}$,
there exists a sequence $(\chi^{(n)}_k)\subset{\mathcal{S}}$ such that
$\|{\mathrm{L}}\chi^{(n)}_k\|_{L_2}\to0$ and
$\lim_{k\to \infty}\chi^{(n)}_k=p_n$ in ${\mathcal{S}}'$.
\item \emph{Riesz representation of the conditioned test functions:} for every
$\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, there exists a unique $e_\varphi\in{\mathcal{R}}$ such that
${\mathrm{L}}^\ast e_\varphi=\iota\varphi$; equivalently, there exists $C_\varphi<\infty$ such that
\begin{align*}
|\langle\psi,\varphi\rangle|\le C_\varphi\,\|{\mathrm{L}}\psi\|_{L_2},
\qquad\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}.
\end{align*}
\item \emph{Sharpness:}
\begin{align*}
\|{\mathrm{L}}\psi\|_{L_2}
\le\sup_{\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\backslash\{0\}}
\frac{|\langle\psi,\varphi\rangle|}{\|e_\varphi\|_{L_2}},
\qquad\psi\in{\mathcal{S}};
\end{align*}
equivalently, the subspace
${\mathcal{E}}:=\{e_\varphi:\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\}$ is dense in ${\mathcal{R}}$.
\end{enumerate}
In this case, ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$; the optimal constant in Item 2 is
$C_\varphi=\|e_\varphi\|_{L_2}=|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}$, where $|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ is the norm that is dual to $\|{\mathrm{L}}\cdot\|_{L_2}$; the form of every
admissible ${\mathrm{A}}$ is
$\langle{\mathrm{A}}\varphi,\psi\rangle=\langle e_\varphi,e_\psi\rangle_{L_2}$ on
${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$; and the extension of $\psi\mapsto{\mathrm{L}}\psi$ is an isometric
isomorphism $\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}:\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}\to{\mathcal{R}}$, so that
$\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}(\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}})={\mathcal{R}}$ and ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}\cong{\mathrm{L}}^\ast({\mathcal{R}})$. Under these
identifications, the representer map $\varphi\mapsto e_\varphi$ from ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ to ${\mathcal{R}}$
coincides with $\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}\circ\pi{\mathrm{A}}$; equivalently, the map
$\pi{\mathrm{A}}:{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'/{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, which extends to the isometry
${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}\to\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$, is given by $\pi{\mathrm{A}}\varphi=\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}^{-1}e_\varphi$
(the representer $e_\varphi$ itself lives in the intermediate space ${\mathcal{R}}$).
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Let us fix a biorthogonal family ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}$ with projectors ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ and
${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast$ (Lemma~\ref{Lem:Biorthogonal}). The strategy is to remove the
conditioning by augmentation, so as to reduce the statement to
Lemma~\ref{Lem:UnconditionedCriterion}.

\medskip
\emph{Augmented factorization}:
We set ${\mathcal{K}}_+:= L_2\oplus \mathbb{R}^{N_0}$ and
\begin{align*}
\Psi:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{K}}_+,
\qquad
\Psi\varphi:=\big({\mathrm{L}}\varphi,{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\varphi)\big),
\end{align*}
which is continuous, and injective because ${\mathrm{L}}$ is. Its adjoint is
$\Psi^\ast(w,{\bf{a}})={\mathrm{L}}^\ast w+\iota\big({\boldsymbol{\phi}}^\mathsf{T}{\bf{a}}\big)$, so that
\begin{align*}
\Psi^\ast\Psi={\mathrm{L}}^\ast{\mathrm{L}}+{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathrm{B}}_+,
\qquad
|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+}=\|\Psi\varphi\|_{{\mathcal{K}}_+} ,
\end{align*}
in the notation of Theorem~\ref{Thm:Augmentation}. The augmented normal operator is therefore
itself a normal operator, for an operator that carries no conditioning. We write
${\mathcal{R}}_\Psi=\overline{\Psi({\mathcal{S}})}^{\,{\mathcal{K}}_+}$.

\medskip
\emph{Conditions 1--3 are the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{Lem:UnconditionedCriterion} for $\Psi$}:
Let us first observe that $\Psi^\ast({\boldsymbol{0}},{\boldsymbol{e}}_n)=\iota\phi_n$, which represents $\phi_n$ at no
cost, and that $\Psi^\ast(e_\varphi,{\boldsymbol{0}})={\mathrm{L}}^\ast e_\varphi=\iota\varphi$ for
$\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ under Condition 2. Since
${\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$, Condition 2 is therefore equivalent to
Condition (a) for $\Psi$, with
\begin{align*}
e^+_\varphi=\big(e_{{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi},\ {\boldsymbol{p}}(\varphi)\big),
\qquad
{\mathcal{E}}_+={\mathcal{E}}\oplus \mathbb{R}^{N_0} .
\end{align*}
Next, the biorthogonality gives ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}(p_n)={\boldsymbol{e}}_n$, so that the sequences of Condition 1
satisfy $\Psi\chi^{(n)}_k\to({\boldsymbol{0}},{\boldsymbol{e}}_n)$, which places $\{{\boldsymbol{0}}\}\oplus \mathbb{R}^{N_0}$ in
${\mathcal{R}}_\Psi$ and yields ${\mathcal{R}}_\Psi={\mathcal{R}}\oplus \mathbb{R}^{N_0}$. Conversely, let us assume
Condition 2 together with $({\boldsymbol{0}},{\boldsymbol{e}}_n)\in{\mathcal{R}}_\Psi$, and let $(\chi_k)\subset{\mathcal{S}}$ be such
that $\|{\mathrm{L}}\chi_k\|_{L_2}\to0$ and ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\chi_k)\to{\boldsymbol{e}}_n$. The decomposition
$\psi={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\psi+{\boldsymbol{\phi}}^\mathsf{T}{\boldsymbol{p}}(\psi)$ and the reproducing identity
\eqref{Eq:Reproducing} give
\begin{align*}
\langle\chi_k,\psi\rangle
=\langle e_{{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\psi},{\mathrm{L}}\chi_k\rangle_{L_2}
+{\boldsymbol{p}}(\psi)^\mathsf{T}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\chi_k)
\longrightarrow
\langle p_n,\psi\rangle,
\qquad\psi\in{\mathcal{S}},
\end{align*}
so that $\chi_k\to p_n$ in ${\mathcal{S}}'$. Condition 1 is thus equivalent to the splitting
${\mathcal{R}}_\Psi={\mathcal{R}}\oplus \mathbb{R}^{N_0}$: it states that the finite-dimensional block is genuinely
present in the concrete dual. Given this splitting, the density of ${\mathcal{E}}_+$ in ${\mathcal{R}}_\Psi$
reduces to the density of ${\mathcal{E}}$ in ${\mathcal{R}}$, so that Condition 3 is equivalent to
Condition (b) for $\Psi$.

\medskip
\emph{Augmented pair}:
Lemma~\ref{Lem:UnconditionedCriterion}, applied to ${\mathrm{T}}=\Psi$, provides a continuous
positive-definite operator ${\mathrm{A}}_+:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ such that $({\mathrm{A}}_+,{\mathrm{B}}_+)$ is a
compatible Riesz pair, with
\begin{align*}
\langle{\mathrm{A}}_+\varphi,\psi\rangle
=\langle e^+_\varphi,e^+_\psi\rangle_{{\mathcal{K}}_+}
=\langle e_{{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi},e_{{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\psi}\rangle_{L_2}
+{\boldsymbol{p}}(\varphi)^\mathsf{T}{\boldsymbol{p}}(\psi).
\end{align*}
This identifies ${\mathrm{A}}_+={\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}+{\mathrm{R}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ in the notation of
Theorem~\ref{Thm:Augmentation}, where the first term is the conditioned Gram operator
${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ of Theorem~\ref{Thm:Concretization}. By
Theorem~\ref{Theo:HilbertPositiveDef}, the completions
${\mathcal{H}}=\overline{({\mathcal{S}},|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{A}}_+})}$ and
${\mathcal{H}}'=\overline{({\mathcal{S}},|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+})}$ are mutually dual Hilbert--Gelfand spaces whose
Riesz maps are the extensions of ${\mathrm{A}}_+$ and ${\mathrm{B}}_+$. All the completion arguments are
inherited from that theorem, and none of them needs to be repeated here.

\medskip
\emph{Descent to $({\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}},{\mathrm{B}})$}:
The block form of the representers gives ${\mathrm{A}}_+\phi_n=p_n$, so that the isometry
${\mathrm{A}}_+:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}'$ maps ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ onto ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$. It therefore maps the
orthogonal complements onto one another,
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{A}}_+:\ {\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}={\mathcal{H}}\ominus{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}
\ \longrightarrow\
{\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}={\mathcal{H}}'\ominus{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}},
\end{align*}
isometrically and onto. The elements of ${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ annihilate ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}$, so that the norm
of ${\mathcal{H}}'$ reduces there to $|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}}$, which identifies ${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ with
$\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ through ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast$ (Theorem~\ref{Thm:Concretization},
Item 1). The restriction of ${\mathrm{A}}_+$ to the primary blocks is thus the isometry
$\pi{\mathrm{A}}:{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}\to\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ of Definition~\ref{Def:Intrinsic}(ii), with
inverse the extension of ${\mathrm{B}}$, while Clause~(i) of that definition is the equivalence
established in the second step. Hence $({\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}},{\mathrm{B}})$ is a ${\boldsymbol{p}}$-conditional Riesz
pair, and Theorem~\ref{Thm:AttainableNull} adds that the conditioning is minimal. The
identifications of the statement are those of Lemma~\ref{Lem:UnconditionedCriterion}, read
through this splitting: $C_\varphi=\|e_\varphi\|_{L_2}=|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}$, the map
$\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}$ is the isometry $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}\to{\mathcal{R}}$, and
$\pi{\mathrm{A}}\varphi=\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}^{-1}e_\varphi$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
\label{Rem:NaiveAnnihilation}
Condition 1 is the attainable form of the requirement ${\mathrm{L}}({\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}})=\{0\}$. The naive
form of this requirement---that the distributional (Fourier-multiplier) extension of ${\mathrm{L}}$
to ${\mathcal{S}}'$ annihilates ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$---is necessary but not sufficient (see
Example~\ref{Ex:FracLaplacian}). For $N_0=0$, the theorem reduces to the characterization of
compatible Riesz pairs generated through $L_2$.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
\label{Rem:SharpnessAsDuality}
Since $\ker({\mathrm{L}}^\ast)={\mathcal{R}}^{\perp}$, Condition 2 is the range inclusion
$\iota({\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}})\subseteq{\mathrm{L}}^\ast({\mathcal{R}})$, with the representer recovered as
$e_\varphi=\big({\mathrm{L}}^\ast|_{{\mathcal{R}}}\big)^{-1}\iota\varphi$; this is the stable inverse
${{\mathrm{L}}^\ast}^{-1}$ that appears in Theorem~\ref{Thm:PracticalChecklist}. Condition 3 is the
sharpness of the resulting bound, and it is equivalent to the density of ${\mathcal{E}}$ in
${\mathcal{R}}$. In practice, only the stated inequality of Condition 3 needs to be verified, because
the reproducing identity \eqref{Eq:Reproducing} yields the reverse one by the
Cauchy--Schwarz inequality. The duality that underlies the two conditions is discussed after
Theorem~\ref{Thm:PracticalChecklist}.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{Cor:L2RealizationConditional}
Let the conditions of Theorem~\ref{Thm:NormalPair} hold. Then, the extension
${\mathrm{T}}:{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}\to{\mathcal{R}}$ of $\varphi\mapsto e_\varphi$ and the extension
$\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}:\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}\to{\mathcal{R}}$ of $\psi\mapsto{\mathrm{L}}\psi$ are isometric isomorphisms
onto ${\mathcal{R}}=\overline{{\mathrm{L}}({\mathcal{S}})}^{\,L_2}$. In particular, the following statements are
equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\ker({\mathrm{L}}^\ast)=\{0\}$;
\item ${\mathrm{L}}({\mathcal{S}})$ is dense in $L_2$;
\item ${\mathrm{T}}:{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}\to L_2$ is an isometric isomorphism;
\item $\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}:\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}\to L_2$ is an isometric isomorphism.
\end{enumerate}
In this case, the inverse of ${\mathrm{T}}$ is the adjoint ${\mathrm{L}}^\ast:L_2\to{\mathcal{S}}'$, which maps
$L_2$ isometrically onto the realized space ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}={\mathrm{L}}^\ast(L_2)$, with
$\|{\mathrm{L}}^\ast w\|_{{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}}=\|w\|_{L_2}$ for every $w\in L_2$. The same criterion governs
the concrete realization
${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast\big(\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}\big)$ of
Theorem~\ref{Thm:Concretization}: since ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast$ is an isometric identification of
$\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ (Theorem~\ref{Thm:Concretization}, Item 1), the composition
${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}^{-1}:L_2\to{\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ is an isometric isomorphism if and only if
Items 1--4 hold. Correspondingly, we also have $L_2=\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}(\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}})$
with $\|{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast f\|_{{\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}}=\|f\|_{\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}=\|\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}} f\|_{L_2}$ for every $f \in \dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$.
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
By the last step of the proof of Theorem~\ref{Thm:NormalPair}, ${\mathrm{T}}$ is the restriction of
${\mathrm{A}}_+$ to the primary blocks, which is an isometry onto ${\mathcal{R}}$, so that
${\mathrm{T}}({\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}})={\mathcal{R}}$; likewise, $\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}$ is the isometric extension of
$\psi\mapsto{\mathrm{L}}\psi$ from the dense subspace ${\mathcal{S}}$ of $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$, with range
${\mathcal{R}}$. Since $\ker({\mathrm{L}}^\ast)={\mathcal{R}}^{\perp}$, each of Items 1--4 is
equivalent to ${\mathcal{R}}=L_2$. The assertion on ${\mathrm{T}}^{-1}$ restates the realization
${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}\cong{\mathrm{L}}^\ast({\mathcal{R}})$ of Theorem~\ref{Thm:NormalPair} with ${\mathcal{R}}=L_2$,
where the injectivity of ${\mathrm{L}}^\ast$ guarantees that the transported norm is well-defined.
The statement on ${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ follows by composing the two isometries.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{Thm:PracticalChecklist}
Let ${\mathrm{L}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to L_2$ be a continuous linear operator, and let
\begin{align*}
Q(\varphi)
:=
\sup_{\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}\backslash\{0\}}
\frac{|\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle|^2}{\|{\mathrm{L}}\psi\|_{L_2}^2}
\in[0,+\infty],
\qquad
\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}},
\end{align*}
denote the dual energy. The following four conditions are each necessary and, together,
sufficient for the existence of a continuous operator ${\mathrm{A}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ such that
$({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{L}}^\ast{\mathrm{L}})$ is a ${\boldsymbol{p}}$-conditional Riesz pair for $({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}')$ whose
canonical maps ${\mathrm{T}}:{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}\to L_2$ and $\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}:\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}\to L_2$ are
isometric isomorphisms.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{(Injectivity)} ${\mathrm{L}}$ is injective on ${\mathcal{S}}$.
\item \emph{(Dense range)} ${\mathrm{L}}({\mathcal{S}})$ is dense in $L_2$; equivalently,
$\ker({\mathrm{L}}^\ast)=\{0\}$.
\item \emph{(Strict ${\boldsymbol{p}}$-conditional positivity)}
\begin{align*}
Q(\varphi)<\infty
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}.
\end{align*}
\item \emph{(Sharpness)}
\begin{align*}
\|{\mathrm{L}}\psi\|_{L_2}
\le\sup_{\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\backslash\{0\}}
\frac{|\langle\psi,\varphi\rangle|}{\sqrt{Q(\varphi)}},
\qquad
\psi\in{\mathcal{S}};
\end{align*}
equivalently, the representers
$e_\varphi:={{\mathrm{L}}^\ast}^{-1}\iota\varphi$, which are well-defined for
$\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ by Conditions 2--3 and satisfy
$\|e_\varphi\|_{L_2}^2=Q(\varphi)$, span a dense subspace of $L_2$.
\end{enumerate}
When Conditions 1--4 hold, the following identifications are valid:
${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$; the quadratic form of every admissible ${\mathrm{A}}$ is $Q$ on
${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, and the conditioned operator that is built from a Green-function inverse of
${\mathrm{L}}^\ast$ is admissible (Corollary~\ref{Cor:GreenFactor}); the stable inverse
${{\mathrm{L}}^\ast}^{-1}$ maps the realized space ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}={\mathrm{L}}^\ast(L_2)$ isometrically
onto $L_2$ and retrieves the representers, $e_\varphi={{\mathrm{L}}^\ast}^{-1}\iota\varphi$; and the
concrete realization ${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast(\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}})$ is isometrically
isomorphic to $L_2$ through ${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}^{-1}$.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Throughout the proof, we set ${\mathcal{R}}=\overline{{\mathrm{L}}({\mathcal{S}})}^{\,L_2}$ and
${\mathcal{D}}:=\{\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}:\ Q(\varphi)<\infty\}$. When ${\mathrm{L}}$ is injective, the
\emph{Representers} step of the proof of Lemma~\ref{Lem:UnconditionedCriterion}, applied to
${\mathrm{T}}={\mathrm{L}}$, states that $Q(\varphi)<\infty$ if and only if there exists a unique $e_\varphi\in{\mathcal{R}}$ with
${\mathrm{L}}^\ast e_\varphi=\iota\varphi$, in which case $Q(\varphi)=\|e_\varphi\|_{L_2}^2$. Hence ${\mathcal{D}}$ is the subspace of test functions that
are representable through ${\mathrm{L}}^\ast$, and Condition 3 splits into the representation
property ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}$ and the minimality property
${\mathcal{D}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$.

\medskip
\emph{Step 1 (Minimality is equivalent to attainability)}:
We claim that, if ${\mathrm{L}}$ is injective and ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}$, then
\begin{align*}
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
{\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}.
\end{align*}
Let us first assume that ${\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, and let $\Psi$ be the map of the proof of
Theorem~\ref{Thm:NormalPair}. A pair $(w,{\bf{a}})\in L_2\oplus \mathbb{R}^{N_0}$ annihilates the range
of $\Psi$ if and only if
$\langle w,{\mathrm{L}}\varphi\rangle_{L_2}+{\bf{a}}^\mathsf{T}{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(\varphi)=0$ for every
$\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}$; that is, ${\mathrm{L}}^\ast w=-\iota\big({\boldsymbol{\phi}}^\mathsf{T}{\bf{a}}\big)$. This
forces ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}^\mathsf{T}{\bf{a}}\in{\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, and the decomposition
${\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ then gives ${\bf{a}}={\boldsymbol{0}}$. The annihilator of
$\Psi({\mathcal{S}})$ is therefore contained in $L_2\oplus\{{\boldsymbol{0}}\}$, so that
${\mathcal{R}}_\Psi\supseteq\{{\boldsymbol{0}}\}\oplus \mathbb{R}^{N_0}$. By the second step of the proof of
Theorem~\ref{Thm:NormalPair}, that splitting is equivalent to Condition 1 of that theorem,
which gives $p_n\in{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ for every $n$.

Conversely, let us assume that ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ and let
$\varphi\in{\mathcal{D}}$. For each $n$, we pick $(\chi_k)\subset{\mathcal{S}}$ with
$\|{\mathrm{L}}\chi_k\|_{L_2}\to0$ and $\chi_k\to p_n$ in ${\mathcal{S}}'$; then
\begin{align*}
\langle p_n,\varphi\rangle
=\lim_k\langle\chi_k,\varphi\rangle
=\lim_k\langle{\mathrm{L}}\chi_k,e_\varphi\rangle_{L_2}
=0,
\end{align*}
so that $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$. Hence ${\mathcal{D}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, and equality follows
from the standing inclusion. This proves the claim.

\medskip
\emph{Step 2 (Sufficiency)}:
Let us assume Conditions 1--4. Condition 1 makes
$|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}}=\|{\mathrm{L}}\cdot\|_{L_2}$ a norm, and Condition 2 gives ${\mathcal{R}}=L_2$. Condition 3
provides the representation property on ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ and, through Step 1, the attainable
annihilation of ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$; Condition 4 is the sharpness requirement. Hence the three
conditions of Theorem~\ref{Thm:NormalPair} are met, which yields an admissible ${\mathrm{A}}$, and
Corollary~\ref{Cor:L2RealizationConditional} upgrades the canonical isometries onto
${\mathcal{R}}=L_2$.

\medskip
\emph{Step 3 (Necessity)}:
Condition 1 is required for ${\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{L}}^\ast{\mathrm{L}}$ to be positive-definite, and Condition 2
is Item 2 of Corollary~\ref{Cor:L2RealizationConditional}. By
Theorem~\ref{Thm:NormalPair}, the representation property
(${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}$), the attainable annihilation, and the sharpness are all
necessary; Step 1 converts the attainable annihilation into
${\mathcal{D}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, so that Condition 3 is necessary, and the sharpness is
Condition 4.

\medskip
\emph{Step 4 (Identifications)}:
These restate the identifications of Theorem~\ref{Thm:NormalPair} and
Corollary~\ref{Cor:L2RealizationConditional}, combined with
$Q(\varphi)=\|e_\varphi\|_{L_2}^2$ on ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
\label{Def:Regular}
A continuous operator ${\mathrm{T}}:{\mathcal{S}}'\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ is said to be \emph{regular} on ${\mathcal{S}}$ if it
maps the test functions among themselves; that is, if ${\mathrm{T}}|_{{\mathcal{S}}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}$.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
\label{Cor:AutomaticSharpness}
Let
${\mathrm{L}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to L_2$ satisfy Conditions 1--3 of Theorem~\ref{Thm:PracticalChecklist}. Then,
for every $\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$ whose image ${\mathrm{B}}\psi={\mathrm{L}}^\ast{\mathrm{L}}\psi$ is a test function, one
has ${\mathrm{B}}\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ and the supremum of Condition 4 is attained at
$\varphi={\mathrm{B}}\psi$, with $e_\varphi={\mathrm{L}}\psi$ and
\begin{align*}
\frac{|\langle\psi,\varphi\rangle|}{\sqrt{Q(\varphi)}}=\|{\mathrm{L}}\psi\|_{L_2} .
\end{align*}
Consequently, if ${\mathrm{L}}$ (or, more generally, ${\mathrm{B}}$) is regular, then Condition 4 holds automatically,
so that Conditions 1--3 alone characterize the admissibility of such operators.
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
\medskip
\emph{Conditioned range}:
Let $\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$ be such that $\varphi:={\mathrm{B}}\psi$ belongs to ${\mathcal{S}}$. Since
${\mathrm{L}}\psi\in L_2$ and ${\mathrm{L}}^\ast({\mathrm{L}}\psi)=\iota\varphi$, the uniqueness of the
representer in \eqref{Eq:RepresenterCriterion} gives $e_\varphi={\mathrm{L}}\psi$, so that
$Q(\varphi)=\|{\mathrm{L}}\psi\|^2_{L_2}<\infty$. Condition 3 then places $\varphi$ in
${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, which is what makes $\varphi$ admissible in the supremum of Condition 4. In
particular, if ${\mathrm{B}}$ is regular, then ${\mathrm{B}}({\mathcal{S}})\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$.

\medskip
\emph{Attainment}:
The reproducing identity \eqref{Eq:Reproducing} gives
$\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle=\|{\mathrm{L}}\psi\|^2_{L_2}$, from which the announced ratio follows.
It attains the Cauchy--Schwarz bound of Remark~\ref{Rem:SharpnessAsDuality}: the supremum of Condition 4 is the norm of the orthogonal projection of
${\mathrm{L}}\psi$ onto $\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}$, so that sharpness is a statement of surjectivity rather
than an inequality.

\medskip
\emph{Regular normal operator}:
Let us now assume that ${\mathrm{B}}$ is regular, so that ${\mathrm{B}}\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$ for every
$\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$. The two preceding steps then produce, for every $\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$, a conditioned
test function $\varphi={\mathrm{B}}\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ with $e_\varphi={\mathrm{L}}\psi$, which gives
${\mathcal{E}}\supseteq{\mathrm{L}}({\mathcal{S}})$. The latter is dense in $L_2$ by Condition 2, and hence so is
${\mathcal{E}}$, which is Condition 4.

\medskip
\emph{Regular ${\mathrm{L}}$}:
The adjoint of ${\mathrm{L}}|_{{\mathcal{S}}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}$ maps ${\mathcal{S}}'$ into ${\mathcal{S}}'$, while the
adjoint of ${\mathrm{L}}:{\mathcal{S}}'\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ maps ${\mathcal{S}}=({\mathcal{S}}')'$ into ${\mathcal{S}}$; the two coincide,
so that ${\mathrm{L}}^\ast$ is regular. The composition of two regular operators being regular, the
normal operator ${\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{L}}^\ast{\mathrm{L}}$ is regular as well.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{Prop:MultiplierConstruction}
Let $({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}')=({\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R}^d),{\mathcal{S}}'( \mathbb{R}^d))$ and let ${\mathrm{L}}$ be the Fourier-multiplier
operator $\widehat{{\mathrm{L}}\varphi}=\hat L\,\hat\varphi$, where the symbol $\hat L: \mathbb{R}^d\to\mathbb{C}$ is
measurable, Hermitian-symmetric ($\hat L(-{\boldsymbol{\omega}})=\overline{\hat L({\boldsymbol{\omega}})}$, so that
${\mathrm{L}}$ maps real functions to real functions), and satisfies, for some order $\gamma>0$, exponents
$\beta,\beta'\ge0$, and positive constants $C,c_1,c_2,c_3$,
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $|\hat L({\boldsymbol{\omega}})|\le C\,(1+\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|)^{\beta}$ for a.e.\ ${\boldsymbol{\omega}}$;
\item[(ii)] $c_1\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|^{\gamma}\le|\hat L({\boldsymbol{\omega}})|\le c_2\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|^{\gamma}$ for
$\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|\le1$;
\item[(iii)] $|\hat L({\boldsymbol{\omega}})|\ge c_3\,(1+\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|)^{-\beta'}$ for $\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|\ge1$.
\end{enumerate}
Let ${\boldsymbol{p}}$ be a basis of the polynomials of degree at most
$k_\gamma=\lfloor\gamma-\frac d2\rfloor$ (no conditioning if $k_\gamma<0$). Then, Conditions
1--4 of Theorem~\ref{Thm:PracticalChecklist} hold, with the dual energy and the representers
given by
\begin{align*}
Q(\varphi)
=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^d}\int_{ \mathbb{R}^d}\frac{|\hat\varphi({\boldsymbol{\omega}})|^2}{|\hat L({\boldsymbol{\omega}})|^2}\,
{\rm d}{\boldsymbol{\omega}},
\qquad
\hat e_\varphi=\frac{\hat\varphi}{\overline{\hat L}}.
\end{align*}
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Condition (i) gives the continuity of ${\mathrm{L}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to L_2$, and Conditions (ii)--(iii) imply
that $\hat L\neq0$ a.e., which yields Conditions 1 and 2 of the checklist; in particular,
${\mathcal{R}}=L_2$.

\medskip
\emph{Dual energy and representers}:
The adjoint ${\mathrm{L}}^\ast$ is the convolution with Fourier symbol $\overline{\hat L}$. For
$e\in L_2$, the product $\overline{\hat L}\,\hat e$ is locally integrable by (i), so that the
representer equation ${\mathrm{L}}^\ast e=\iota\varphi$ of \eqref{Eq:RepresenterCriterion} is an
identity between locally integrable functions; namely,
\begin{align*}
\overline{\hat L}({\boldsymbol{\omega}})\,\hat e({\boldsymbol{\omega}})=\hat\varphi({\boldsymbol{\omega}})
\qquad\text{for a.e. }{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\in \mathbb{R}^d .
\end{align*}
Since $\hat L\neq0$ a.e., this equation admits the single measurable solution
$\hat e=\hat\varphi/\overline{\hat L}$, which lies in $L_2$ if and only if the integral
$\int_{ \mathbb{R}^d}|\hat\varphi|^2/|\hat L|^2\,{\rm d}{\boldsymbol{\omega}}$ converges. The criterion
\eqref{Eq:RepresenterCriterion} of Lemma~\ref{Lem:UnconditionedCriterion}, applied with
${\mathrm{T}}={\mathrm{L}}$, therefore delivers the dual energy at once:
\begin{align*}
\hat e_\varphi=\frac{\hat\varphi}{\overline{\hat L}},
\qquad
Q(\varphi)
=\|e_\varphi\|_{L_2}^2
=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^d}\int_{ \mathbb{R}^d}
\frac{|\hat\varphi({\boldsymbol{\omega}})|^2}{|\hat L({\boldsymbol{\omega}})|^2}\,{\rm d}{\boldsymbol{\omega}} ,
\end{align*}
where both sides are infinite when the integral diverges, the representer equation having then
no solution in $L_2$. Remarkably, neither a supremum nor a limiting argument is required.

\medskip
\emph{Finiteness domain}:
The integral converges over
$\{\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|\ge1\}$ for every $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}$, by (iii). Near the origin, the two-sided
bound (ii) shows that it converges if and only if
$\int_{\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|\le1}\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|^{-2\gamma}|\hat\varphi|^2\,{\rm d}{\boldsymbol{\omega}}<\infty$: if the
moments of $\varphi$ of order at most $k_\gamma$ vanish, then
$|\hat\varphi({\boldsymbol{\omega}})|\lesssim\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|^{k_\gamma+1}$ and the radial exponent
$2(k_\gamma+1)-2\gamma+d-1$ exceeds $-1$; if a moment of order $j\le k_\gamma$ survives, then
the leading Taylor term of $\hat\varphi$ has order $j$ and forces divergence, because
$2j-2\gamma+d\le0$. Hence $\{Q<\infty\}={\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, which is Condition 3.

\medskip
\emph{Sharpness}:
A multiplier is regular in the sense of Definition~\ref{Def:Regular} exactly when its symbol
belongs to the space ${\mathcal{O}}_M( \mathbb{R}^d)$ of smooth functions whose derivatives are all of at most
polynomial growth, which is the classical space of multipliers of ${\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R}^d)$
\cite{Treves2006}. Whenever $\hat L$ or $|\hat L|^2$ is of that type,
Corollary~\ref{Cor:AutomaticSharpness} applies and Condition 4 requires no verification: such is
the case for the polynomial symbols $P(\mathrm{i}\omega)$ of Example~\ref{Ex:ExpSplines}, and for the
fractional Laplacians of integer order $\gamma$ (Example~\ref{Ex:FracLaplacian}), whose symbol
$\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|^\gamma$ is smooth only for even $\gamma$, while
$|\hat L|^2=\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|^{2\gamma}$ is a polynomial for every integer $\gamma$.

For a general measurable symbol, we approximate the maximizer $\varphi={\mathrm{B}}\psi$ of
Corollary~\ref{Cor:AutomaticSharpness}, whose transform is $|\hat L|^2\hat\psi$. Let
$\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$ and let $A_k=\{1/k<\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|<k\}$ be the open annulus, on whose closure the
bounds (i)--(iii) confine $|\hat L|$ between two positive constants $c_k$ and $C_k$. For each
$k$, we select $\hat\varphi_k\in C^\infty_c(A_k)$ with
$\big\|\hat\varphi_k-|\hat L|^2\hat\psi\,\mathbbm{1}_{A_k}\big\|_{L_2}\le c_k/k$, which is
possible because $|\hat L|^2\hat\psi\,\mathbbm{1}_{A_k}$ is bounded and supported in $A_k$. The
vanishing of $\hat\varphi_k$ near the origin annihilates all the moments of $\varphi_k$, so
that $\varphi_k\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, and
\begin{align*}
\big\|\hat e_{\varphi_k}-\hat L\hat\psi\big\|_{L_2}
&\le\frac{1}{c_k}
\big\|\hat\varphi_k-|\hat L|^2\hat\psi\,\mathbbm{1}_{A_k}\big\|_{L_2}
+\big\|\hat L\hat\psi\,\mathbbm{1}_{ \mathbb{R}^d\backslash A_k}\big\|_{L_2}\\
&\le\frac1k
+\big\|\hat L\hat\psi\,\mathbbm{1}_{ \mathbb{R}^d\backslash A_k}\big\|_{L_2},
\end{align*}
where the choice of the tolerance $c_k/k$ compensates for the degradation of $1/c_k$ as the
annulus grows. The right-hand side tends to $0$, its second term because
$\hat L\hat\psi\in L_2$ and $\mathbbm{1}_{A_k}\to1$ a.e. Therefore
${\mathrm{L}}\psi\in\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}$ for every $\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$: the orthogonal projection in the
supremum of Condition 4 is lossless, which is the announced sharpness.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{Prop:Factorization}
Let Conditions 1--4 of Theorem~\ref{Thm:PracticalChecklist} hold and let us define the
\emph{conditioned inverse} of ${\mathrm{L}}$ as
\begin{align}
\label{Eq:CondInverse}
{\mathrm{L}}^{-1}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}
:=
{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\,{\mathrm{L}}^\ast:\ L_2\to{\mathcal{S}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}' .
\end{align}
Then, the following properties hold.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Inversion}: ${\mathrm{L}}^{-1}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\,{\mathrm{L}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast$ on ${\mathcal{S}}$ and
${\mathrm{L}}\,{\mathrm{L}}^{-1}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}=\mathrm{Id}$ on $L_2$. Moreover, ${\mathrm{L}}^{-1}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ is an
isometric isomorphism from $L_2$ onto ${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}$.
\item \emph{Adjoint}: the adjoint
${\mathrm{L}}^{-1\ast}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathrm{L}}\,{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to L_2$ is the representer map
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{L}}^{-1\ast}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi
=
e_{{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi}
=
{{\mathrm{L}}^\ast}^{-1}\iota({\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi)\in L_2 ,
\end{align*}
with $\|{\mathrm{L}}^{-1\ast}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi\|_{L_2}^2=Q({\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi)$ and
$\ker{\mathrm{L}}^{-1\ast}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$.
\item \emph{Factorization}: the conditional inverse admits the factorization
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}
=
{\mathrm{L}}^{-1}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\circ{\mathrm{L}}^{-1\ast}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}:
{\mathcal{S}}\to L_2 \to {\mathcal{S}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}',
\end{align*}
in exact duality with the factorization ${\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{L}}^\ast{\mathrm{L}}$ of the normal operator.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
\medskip
\emph{Inversion}:
For $\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$, Theorem~\ref{Thm:Concretization} gives
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{L}}^{-1}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{L}}\psi
={\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\,{\mathrm{L}}^\ast{\mathrm{L}}\,\psi
={\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{B}}\psi
={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast\psi .
\end{align*}
The extension of ${\mathrm{L}}$ annihilates ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, so that ${\mathrm{L}}{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast={\mathrm{L}}$;
the application of ${\mathrm{L}}$ to the identity above therefore yields
${\mathrm{L}}{\mathrm{L}}^{-1}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{L}}\psi={\mathrm{L}}\psi$, which extends to $L_2$ by the density of
${\mathrm{L}}({\mathcal{S}})$ (Condition 2). By Corollary~\ref{Cor:L2RealizationConditional}, the adjoint
${\mathrm{L}}^\ast$ maps $L_2$ isometrically onto ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}={\mathrm{L}}^\ast(L_2)$, while
${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ extends to an isometry of ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ onto
${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast(\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}})$ (Theorem~\ref{Thm:Concretization},
Item 1). The composition ${\mathrm{L}}^{-1}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{L}}^\ast$ is thus an isometric
isomorphism from $L_2$ onto ${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}$.

\medskip
\emph{Adjoint}:
The operator ${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ is symmetric (Remark~\ref{Rem:AutomaticSymmetry}) and the adjoint
operation is involutive, from which we get
${\mathrm{L}}^{-1\ast}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}=({\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{L}}^\ast)^\ast={\mathrm{L}}\,{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$. Let
$\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}$. Theorem~\ref{Thm:Concretization} (Item 2) gives
$\pi{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi=\pi{\mathrm{A}}({\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi)$, while
Theorem~\ref{Thm:NormalPair} gives $\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}\circ\pi{\mathrm{A}}={\mathrm{T}}$ on ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$;
hence ${\mathrm{L}}^{-1\ast}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi=e_{{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi}$, whose squared $L_2$-norm is
$Q({\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi)$ by Theorem~\ref{Thm:PracticalChecklist}. Its kernel is
$\ker{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$, because $\varphi\mapsto e_\varphi$ is injective on
${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$.

\medskip
\emph{Factorization}:
The range of ${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ lies in ${\mathcal{S}}'_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$, so that
${\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$. Together with the inversion identity
${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast$ of Theorem~\ref{Thm:Concretization}, this yields
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{L}}^{-1}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\,{\mathrm{L}}^{-1\ast}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}
={\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\,{\mathrm{L}}^\ast{\mathrm{L}}\,{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}
={\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\,{\mathrm{B}}\,{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}
={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast\,{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}
={\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}} ,
\end{align*}
which is the announced factorization.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
\label{Rem:FactorizationConsequences}
The factorization exhibits ${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ as a Gram operator, hence manifestly
symmetric, positive-semi-definite on all of ${\mathcal{S}}$, with null space ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}=\ker{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ and
with quadratic form
$\langle{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi,\varphi\rangle=\|e_{{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi}\|_{L_2}^2
=Q({\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi)$.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- corollary-6: body_md -->
\label{Cor:GreenFactor}
Let Conditions 1--4 of Theorem~\ref{Thm:PracticalChecklist} hold, and let us assume that
${\mathrm{L}}^\ast$ admits a Green-function inverse; that is, a continuous operator
${\mathrm{G}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ such that ${\mathrm{G}}{\mathrm{L}}^\ast=\mathrm{Id}$ on $L_2$ and
${\mathrm{L}}^\ast{\mathrm{G}}=\mathrm{Id}$ on ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ through the
Hilbertian extensions, so that ${\mathrm{G}}=({\mathrm{L}}^\ast)^{-1}$; its adjoint ${\mathrm{G}}^\ast$ is then the
Green's operator of ${\mathrm{L}}$. If
\begin{align}
\label{Eq:L2inclusion}
{\mathrm{G}}({\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}})\subseteq L_2,
\end{align}
then ${\mathrm{G}}\varphi=e_\varphi={{\mathrm{L}}^\ast}^{-1}\iota\varphi$ for every
$\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, so that ${\mathrm{G}}{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathrm{L}}^{-1\ast}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ on ${\mathcal{S}}$.
Consequently, the conditioned Gram operator
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}
:=
{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast\,{\mathrm{G}}^\ast{\mathrm{G}}\,{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}
=({\mathrm{G}}{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})^\ast\,({\mathrm{G}}{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}):{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'
\end{align*}
is well defined and admissible, and it coincides with the canonical factorization
${\mathrm{L}}^{-1}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{L}}^{-1\ast}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ of Proposition~\ref{Prop:Factorization}, in
exact duality with the factorization ${\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{L}}^\ast{\mathrm{L}}$ of the normal operator. In the
Fourier-multiplier setting of Proposition~\ref{Prop:MultiplierConstruction}, ${\mathrm{G}}$ is the
multiplier operator with symbol $1/\overline{\hat L}$, and the requirement
${\mathrm{G}}({\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}})\subseteq L_2$ is met by the canonical normalization of that multiplier,
for which $\|{\mathrm{G}}\varphi\|_{L_2}^2=Q(\varphi)$, whose finiteness on ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ is
Condition 3.
<!-- end corollary-6 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
\medskip
\emph{Identification of the factor}:
Let $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ and $w:={\mathrm{G}}\varphi\in L_2$. The inversion property, read
through the Hilbertian extensions, gives ${\mathrm{L}}^\ast w={\mathrm{L}}^\ast{\mathrm{G}}\varphi=\iota\varphi
={\mathrm{L}}^\ast e_\varphi$. Since $\ker({\mathrm{L}}^\ast)=\{0\}$ by Condition 2, we
conclude that $w=e_\varphi$. The identity ${\mathrm{G}}{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathrm{L}}^{-1\ast}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ then follows
from Proposition~\ref{Prop:Factorization}, which gives
${\mathrm{L}}^{-1\ast}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi=e_{{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi}$.

\medskip
\emph{Conditioned Gram operator}:
The map ${\mathrm{G}}{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ sends ${\mathcal{S}}$ into $L_2$, which makes the composition with the adjoint
well defined. For $\varphi,\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$,
\begin{align*}
\langle{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast{\mathrm{G}}^\ast{\mathrm{G}}\,{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi,\psi\rangle
=\langle{\mathrm{G}}{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi,{\mathrm{G}}{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\psi\rangle_{L_2}
=\langle e_{{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\varphi},e_{{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\psi}\rangle_{L_2},
\end{align*}
which is the quadratic form of the admissible operator that
Lemma~\ref{Lem:UnconditionedCriterion} attaches to ${\mathrm{T}}=\Psi$, as well as the form of the factorization
${\mathrm{L}}^{-1}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{L}}^{-1\ast}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ of Proposition~\ref{Prop:Factorization}; the
three operators therefore coincide.

\medskip
\emph{Multiplier case}:
The symbol of ${\mathrm{G}}$ is $1/\overline{\hat L}$, so that ${\mathrm{G}}\varphi=e_\varphi$ and
$\|{\mathrm{G}}\varphi\|_{L_2}^2=Q(\varphi)$ by
Proposition~\ref{Prop:MultiplierConstruction}, which is finite on ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ by
Condition 3.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
\label{Rem:GreenNormalization}
The inclusion \eqref{Eq:L2inclusion} constrains the choice of ${\mathrm{G}}$ and not the
operator ${\mathrm{L}}$: it fixes the additive ambiguity of the fundamental solution, and the canonical
one meets it. What it excludes are exotic alternatives, such as
$\rho_{{\mathrm{L}}^\ast}(x)=-\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\ln\|{\boldsymbol{x}}\|+(x_1^2-x_2^2)$ for ${\mathrm{L}}=-\Delta$ on ${\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R}^2)$, whose
harmonic correction of degree $2$ survives the moment conditions of order at most $1$. The
conditioning itself is unavoidable: since ${\mathrm{G}}$ maps the complement of ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ outside of
$L_2$, the unconditioned composition ${\mathrm{G}}^\ast{\mathrm{G}}$ is ill-defined. Proposition~\ref{Prop:Factorization}
and Corollary~\ref{Cor:GreenFactor} operate in opposite directions: the former decomposes a given
${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ into the analysis--synthesis pair $({\mathrm{L}}^{-1\ast}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}},{\mathrm{L}}^{-1}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})$,
whereas the latter produces ${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ from a solution $e_\varphi=\rho_{{\mathrm{L}}^\ast}\ast\varphi$
of the representer equation ${\mathrm{L}}^\ast e=\iota\varphi$, which is available in closed form for the
Fourier multipliers of Proposition~\ref{Prop:MultiplierConstruction}.
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{Prop:NativeUniqueness}
Let ${\mathrm{L}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to L_2$ satisfy Conditions 1--4 of Theorem~\ref{Thm:PracticalChecklist}, let ${\boldsymbol{p}}$ be a basis of ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, and let ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}$ be the native space \eqref{Eq:NativeSpace} for some family ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}$ that is biorthogonal to ${\boldsymbol{p}}$. Then, the following properties hold.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Extension of ${\mathrm{L}}$}: The map $\psi\mapsto{\mathrm{L}}\psi$ extends by continuity from ${\mathcal{S}}$ to a bounded operator ${\mathrm{L}}:{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}\to L_2$, which is given by
\begin{align}
\label{Eq:LExtension}
{\mathrm{L}} f=\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}\,\pi f,\qquad f\in{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}},
\end{align}
where $\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}:\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}\to L_2$ is the isometric isomorphism of Theorem~\ref{Thm:PracticalChecklist}. This extension is surjective, its null space is ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, and $|f|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+}^2=\|{\mathrm{L}} f\|_{L_2}^2+|{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(f)|_2^2$ for every $f\in{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}$. Moreover, ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}=\{f\in{\mathcal{S}}':\ \pi f\in\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}\}$ does not depend on ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}$, and two admissible choices of ${\boldsymbol{\phi}}$ yield equivalent norms (Corollary~\ref{Cor:PhiIndependence}).
\item \emph{Uniqueness as a set}: Let ${\mathcal{X}}$ be a Hilbert--Gelfand space such that $\psi\mapsto{\mathrm{L}}\psi$ extends from ${\mathcal{S}}$ to a bounded operator ${\mathrm{L}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}:{\mathcal{X}}\to L_2$ that is surjective. Then, ${\mathcal{X}}={\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}$ as sets, ${\mathrm{L}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}$ coincides with the extension \eqref{Eq:LExtension}, and $\|\cdot\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}$ is equivalent to $|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+}$.
\item \emph{Characterization of the norms \eqref{Eq:NativeSpace}}: Let ${\mathcal{X}}$ be as in Item 2, and let ${\mathcal{X}}'$ be its dual, which is a Hilbert--Gelfand space whose pairing with ${\mathcal{X}}$ extends the canonical one (Theorem~\ref{Theo:HilbertPositiveDef}). Then, $\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}=\|{\mathrm{L}} f\|_{L_2}$ for every $f$ in the orthogonal complement ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}^{\perp}$ of ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ in ${\mathcal{X}}$ if and only if there exist a basis $\tilde{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ of ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ and a family $\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\subset{\mathcal{X}}'$ with $\langle\tilde p_m,\tilde\phi_n\rangle=\delta_{mn}$ such that
\begin{align}
\label{Eq:AugmentedNormGeneral}
\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}^2=\|{\mathrm{L}} f\|_{L_2}^2+|\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}(f)|_2^2,\qquad f\in{\mathcal{X}}.
\end{align}
In that case, the map $f\mapsto({\mathrm{L}} f,\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}(f))$ is a unitary isomorphism from ${\mathcal{X}}$ onto $L_2\times \mathbb{R}^{N_0}$. When $\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}$, the space ${\mathcal{X}}$ is the native space \eqref{Eq:NativeSpace} that is associated with the biorthogonal pair $(\tilde{{\boldsymbol{p}}},\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
\emph{Extension of ${\mathrm{L}}$}:
For $\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$, we have that $\|{\mathrm{L}}\psi\|_{L_2}=|\psi|_{{\mathrm{B}}}=\|\pi\psi\|_{\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}\le|\psi|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+}$, so that $\psi\mapsto{\mathrm{L}}\psi$ extends by continuity to ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}$. The operator $\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}\pi$ is bounded on ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}$, because Item 3 of Theorem~\ref{Thm:Augmentation} gives $\|\pi f\|_{\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}=\|{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast f\|_{{\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}}\le|f|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+}$, and it coincides with ${\mathrm{L}}$ on ${\mathcal{S}}$. The two bounded operators therefore agree on the dense subspace ${\mathcal{S}}$, which proves \eqref{Eq:LExtension}. The map $\pi$ is onto $\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ by \eqref{Eq:CanonicalHprime} and $\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}$ is onto $L_2$ by Condition 2 of Theorem~\ref{Thm:PracticalChecklist}, so that the extension is surjective. Since $\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}$ is injective, its null space is $\ker\pi\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$. The norm identity is Item 3 of Theorem~\ref{Thm:Augmentation}, in which we substitute $\|{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast f\|_{{\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}}=\|\pi f\|_{\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}}=\|\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}\pi f\|_{L_2}$. The last assertion is Corollary~\ref{Cor:PhiIndependence}.

\medskip
\emph{Inclusion ${\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}$}:
Let $f\in{\mathcal{X}}$ and let $(\psi_k)\subset{\mathcal{S}}$ converge to $f$ in ${\mathcal{X}}$. Then, ${\mathrm{L}}\psi_k\to{\mathrm{L}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}f$ in $L_2$, so that $(\psi_k)$ is $|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}}$-Cauchy and $\pi\psi_k$ converges to some $g\in\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ with $\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}g={\mathrm{L}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}f$. By Item 3 of Proposition~\ref{Prop:Realization}, this convergence also takes place in ${\mathcal{S}}'/{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$. On the other hand, $\psi_k\to f$ in ${\mathcal{S}}'$ because ${\mathcal{X}}$ is continuously embedded in ${\mathcal{S}}'$, so that $\pi\psi_k\to\pi f$ in ${\mathcal{S}}'/{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$. Hence $\pi f=g\in\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$, which places $f$ in ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}$ by \eqref{Eq:CanonicalHprime}, and ${\mathrm{L}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}f=\widetilde{{\mathrm{L}}}\pi f={\mathrm{L}} f$. The inclusion map ${\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}$ is continuous: it is a linear map between two Hilbert spaces that are both continuously embedded in the Hausdorff space ${\mathcal{S}}'$, so that its graph is closed, and the closed-graph theorem applies.

\medskip
\emph{Equality ${\mathcal{X}}={\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}$}:
Since ${\mathrm{L}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}:{\mathcal{X}}\to L_2$ is bounded and surjective, its restriction to the orthogonal complement of $\ker{\mathrm{L}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}$ is a bounded bijection onto $L_2$. By the open-mapping theorem, the inverse of this restriction is a bounded operator ${\mathrm{R}}:L_2\to{\mathcal{X}}$ with ${\mathrm{L}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}{\mathrm{R}}=\mathrm{Id}$. For $f\in{\mathcal{X}}$, we have that $f-{\mathrm{R}}{\mathrm{L}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}f\in\ker{\mathrm{L}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\cap{\mathcal{X}}$, so that
\begin{align*}
{\mathcal{X}}={\mathrm{R}}(L_2)+({\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\cap{\mathcal{X}}).
\end{align*}
The range ${\mathrm{R}}(L_2)$ is closed in ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}$. Indeed, the previous step gives the bound $|{\mathrm{R}}w|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+}\le C\|{\mathrm{R}}w\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}\le C\,\|{\mathrm{R}}\|\,\|w\|_{L_2}$, while Item 1 gives $\|w\|_{L_2}=\|{\mathrm{L}}{\mathrm{R}}w\|_{L_2}\le|{\mathrm{R}}w|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+}$. Hence, ${\mathrm{R}}$ is an isomorphism from $L_2$ onto its range in ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}$. The sum of a closed subspace and of a finite-dimensional subspace is closed. Consequently, ${\mathcal{X}}$ is a closed subspace of ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}$ that contains ${\mathcal{S}}$, which is dense in ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}$. We conclude that ${\mathcal{X}}={\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}$. The identity map from $({\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}},\|\cdot\|_{{\mathcal{X}}})$ onto $({\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}},|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+})$ is a continuous bijection between Hilbert spaces, so that its inverse is continuous as well, which is the equivalence of norms.

\medskip
\emph{Characterization of the norms}:
Let us first suppose that $\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}=\|{\mathrm{L}} f\|_{L_2}$ on ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}^\perp$. By Item 2, ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ is a subspace of ${\mathcal{X}}$ of dimension $N_0$; let ${\mathrm{Q}}$ be the orthogonal projector onto it and let $\tilde{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=(\tilde p_1,\dots,\tilde p_{N_0})$ be an orthonormal basis of ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ in ${\mathcal{X}}$. The Riesz representatives $\tilde\phi_n:=\langle\cdot,\tilde p_n\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}}$ belong to ${\mathcal{X}}'$ and satisfy $\langle\tilde p_m,\tilde\phi_n\rangle=\langle\tilde p_m,\tilde p_n\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}}=\delta_{mn}$, while ${\mathrm{Q}}f=\sum_n\langle f,\tilde\phi_n\rangle\tilde p_n$. Since ${\mathrm{L}}$ annihilates ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, we obtain that
\begin{align*}
\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}^2=\|f-{\mathrm{Q}}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}^2+\|{\mathrm{Q}}f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}^2=\|{\mathrm{L}} f\|_{L_2}^2+|\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}(f)|_2^2,
\end{align*}
which is \eqref{Eq:AugmentedNormGeneral}. Conversely, let \eqref{Eq:AugmentedNormGeneral} hold. By polarization, the inner product of ${\mathcal{X}}$ is $\langle f,g\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}}=\langle{\mathrm{L}} f,{\mathrm{L}} g\rangle_{L_2}+\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}(f)^\mathsf{T}\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}(g)$. Since ${\mathrm{L}}\tilde p_m=0$ and by biorthogonality, $\langle f,\tilde p_m\rangle_{{\mathcal{X}}}=\langle f,\tilde\phi_m\rangle$, so that ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}^\perp=\bigcap_n\ker\tilde\phi_n$, on which \eqref{Eq:AugmentedNormGeneral} reduces to $\|f\|_{{\mathcal{X}}}=\|{\mathrm{L}} f\|_{L_2}$. The unitarity of $f\mapsto({\mathrm{L}} f,\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}(f))$ is \eqref{Eq:AugmentedNormGeneral} together with the surjectivity of ${\mathrm{L}}$ and the fact that $\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ takes every value in $ \mathbb{R}^{N_0}$ on ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$. Finally, when $\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}$, the right-hand side of \eqref{Eq:AugmentedNormGeneral} is the norm $|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{B}}_+}$ of \eqref{Eq:NativeSpace} for the pair $(\tilde{{\boldsymbol{p}}},\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}})$, and ${\mathcal{X}}$ is its completion because ${\mathcal{S}}$ is dense in ${\mathcal{X}}$.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- example-2: body_md -->
\label{Ex:FracLaplacian}
Let $({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}')=({\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R}^d),{\mathcal{S}}'( \mathbb{R}^d))$ and let
$(-\Delta)^{\gamma/2}$ be the fractional Laplacian of order $\gamma>0$, which is the self-adjoint
Fourier-multiplier operator specified by
\begin{align*}
\widehat{(-\Delta)^{\gamma/2}\varphi}({\boldsymbol{\omega}})
=\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|^{\gamma}\,\hat\varphi({\boldsymbol{\omega}}),
\end{align*}
where $\hat\varphi({\boldsymbol{\omega}})=\int_{ \mathbb{R}^d}\varphi({\boldsymbol{x}})\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}{\boldsymbol{\omega}}^\mathsf{T}{\boldsymbol{x}}}\,{\rm d}{\boldsymbol{x}}$ and
$\|\cdot\|$ denotes the Euclidean norm. We set
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{B}}
=\big((-\Delta)^{\gamma/2}\big)^{\ast}(-\Delta)^{\gamma/2}
=(-\Delta)^{\gamma},
\qquad
\widehat{{\mathrm{B}}\varphi}({\boldsymbol{\omega}})=\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|^{2\gamma}\,\hat\varphi({\boldsymbol{\omega}}).
\end{align*}
Unless $\gamma$ is an even integer, the symbol $\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|^{\gamma}$ is not smooth at the
origin, so that $(-\Delta)^{\gamma/2}$ does not map ${\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R}^d)$ into itself; the operator
${\mathrm{B}}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ is nevertheless continuous, which is all that the framework requires.
It is symmetric and positive-definite, because
\begin{align*}
\langle{\mathrm{B}}\varphi,\varphi\rangle
=\|(-\Delta)^{\gamma/2}\varphi\|_{L_2}^2
=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^d}\int_{ \mathbb{R}^d}\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|^{2\gamma}\,|\hat\varphi({\boldsymbol{\omega}})|^2\,{\rm d}{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
>0
\end{align*}
for $\varphi\neq0$, with the induced norm
$|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{B}}}=\|(-\Delta)^{\gamma/2}\varphi\|_{L_2}$. We write $\Pi_k$ for the space of
polynomials of degree at most $k$ on $ \mathbb{R}^d$, with the convention that $\Pi_k=\{0\}$ for $k<0$. The
linear-spline setting of Example~\ref{Ex:LinearSpline} is the case $(\gamma,d)=(1,1)$; the value
$\gamma=2$, for which $(-\Delta)^{\gamma/2}=-\Delta$ and ${\mathrm{B}}=\Delta^2$, leads to the thin-plate
setting that we work out at the end of this example.

\medskip
\emph{Distributional null spaces versus the attainable null space}:
Over ${\mathcal{S}}'$, the operator ${\mathrm{B}}$ annihilates every polynomial of degree less than $2\gamma$ and,
for integer $\gamma$ and $d\ge2$, the infinite-dimensional space of $\gamma$-polyharmonic polynomials.
None of these spaces can serve as ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$: the object that the theory calls for is the
attainable null space ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}\subseteq\ker({\mathrm{B}}:{\mathcal{S}}'\to{\mathcal{S}}')$ of
Definition~\ref{Def:AttainableNull}.

\medskip
\emph{Admissibility and the attainable null space}:
The symbol $\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|^{\gamma}$ satisfies conditions (i)--(iii) of
Proposition~\ref{Prop:MultiplierConstruction} with order $\gamma$. Consequently, Conditions
1--4 of Theorem~\ref{Thm:PracticalChecklist} hold with ${\boldsymbol{p}}$ a basis of $\Pi_{k_\gamma}$
(e.g., the monomials of degree at most $k_\gamma$), and the theorem identifies the attainable
null space as
\begin{align*}
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=\Pi_{k_\gamma},
\qquad
k_\gamma:=\Big\lfloor\gamma-\frac d2\Big\rfloor,
\end{align*}
so that ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ is nontrivial only for $\gamma\ge d/2$, with
$N_0=\dim\Pi_{k_\gamma}=\binom{k_\gamma+d}{d}$ and
\begin{align*}
{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}
=\{\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R}^d):\
\text{the moments of $\varphi$ of order at most $k_\gamma$ vanish}\}.
\end{align*}
The dual energy is the explicit integral
\begin{align*}
Q(\varphi)
=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^d}\int_{ \mathbb{R}^d}\frac{|\hat\varphi({\boldsymbol{\omega}})|^2}{\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|^{2\gamma}}\,{\rm d}{\boldsymbol{\omega}},
\end{align*}
whose finiteness domain is decided by the Taylor expansion of $\hat\varphi$ at the origin (see
the proof of Proposition~\ref{Prop:MultiplierConstruction}).

\medskip
\emph{Attainability made explicit}:
It is instructive to exhibit the annihilation of $\Pi_{k_\gamma}$ directly, without the
abstract cutoff construction of Theorem~\ref{Thm:PracticalChecklist}. Let
$\theta\in{\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R}^d)$ be equal to $1$ in a neighborhood of the origin and consider, for a
monomial ${\boldsymbol{x}}^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}$ with $|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}|=j\le k_\gamma$, the dilated products
$\varphi_t({\boldsymbol{x}})={\boldsymbol{x}}^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\,\theta({\boldsymbol{x}}/t)$, whose Fourier transforms are
$\hat\varphi_t({\boldsymbol{\omega}})=\mathrm{i}^{\,j}t^{d+j}h_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(t{\boldsymbol{\omega}})$ with
$h_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}:={\mathrm{D}}^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\hat\theta\in{\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R}^d)$. A change of variables gives
\begin{align*}
|\varphi_t|_{{\mathrm{B}}}^2
=
t^{\,d+2j-2\gamma}\,\frac{1}{(2\pi)^d}\int_{ \mathbb{R}^d}\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\|^{2\gamma}|h_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}({\boldsymbol{u}})|^2\,{\rm d}{\boldsymbol{u}} .
\end{align*}
If $j<\gamma-\frac d2$, then $|\varphi_t|_{{\mathrm{B}}}\to0$ as $t\to\infty$, while
$\varphi_t({\boldsymbol{x}})\to{\boldsymbol{x}}^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}$ in ${\mathcal{S}}'$; hence ${\boldsymbol{x}}^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\in{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$. In the
borderline case $j=\gamma-\frac d2$, which occurs when $\gamma-\frac d2$ is a nonnegative
integer (so that $j=k_\gamma$), the dilations must be averaged logarithmically: the sequence
$\varphi_K({\boldsymbol{x}}):=(\ln K)^{-1}\int_1^K{\boldsymbol{x}}^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\,\theta({\boldsymbol{x}}/t)\,t^{-1}\,{\rm d} t$
converges to ${\boldsymbol{x}}^{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}$ in ${\mathcal{S}}'$, and a Cauchy--Schwarz estimate of the
cross-terms gives $|\varphi_K|_{{\mathrm{B}}}^2\lesssim(\ln K)^{-1}\to0$, because $2\gamma=d+2j$.

\medskip
\emph{The conditional inverse and the order of conditional positivity}:
There is no need to pick an explicit biorthogonal family: its existence is granted by
Lemma~\ref{Lem:Biorthogonal} (a concrete choice consists of Hermite functions; that is, of
suitable linear combinations of the derivatives of a normalized Gaussian). The canonical
fundamental solution of ${\mathrm{B}}$ is the radial kernel
\begin{align*}
g_\gamma({\boldsymbol{x}})
=
\begin{cases}
c_{\gamma,d}\,\|{\boldsymbol{x}}\|^{2\gamma-d}, & \gamma-\frac d2\notin\mathbb{Z}_{\ge0},\\[4pt]
c_{\gamma,d}\,\|{\boldsymbol{x}}\|^{2\gamma-d}\ln\|{\boldsymbol{x}}\|, & \gamma-\frac d2\in\mathbb{Z}_{\ge0},
\end{cases}
\end{align*}
with known normalization constants $c_{\gamma,d}$ \cite{Samko1993,Wendland2005}; its symbol is $\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|^{-2\gamma}$,
and we note that the logarithmic branch occurs exactly when the borderline degree
$k_\gamma=\gamma-\frac d2$ is present in ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$. We set
${\mathrm{A}}=g_\gamma\ast\cdot$, which is a two-sided Green inverse of ${\mathrm{B}}$, with
${\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{B}}=\mathrm{Id}$ on ${\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R}^d)$ and ${\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{A}}=\mathrm{Id}$ on ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$
through the Hilbertian extensions. By Corollary~\ref{Cor:GreenNormal}, $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}})$ is a
${\boldsymbol{p}}$-conditional Riesz pair with the \emph{minimal} conditioning
(Theorem~\ref{Thm:AttainableNull}); for $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, the Parseval relation
identifies its quadratic form with the dual energy,
$\langle{\mathrm{A}}\varphi,\varphi\rangle=Q(\varphi)$, and
${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast{\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$ is admissible (Corollary~\ref{Cor:GreenFactor}). In
the classical terminology of radial basis functions, $k_\gamma+1$ is the order of
conditional positive-definiteness of the kernel $g_\gamma({\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{y}})$
\cite{Madych1990c,Wendland2005}.
The ambiguity of the fundamental solution is the trace of the distributional kernel of
${\mathrm{B}}$: additive polynomial corrections of degree at most $2k_\gamma+1$ are harmless, because
they map ${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ into ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ and alter neither the class $\pi{\mathrm{A}}\varphi$ nor the
quadratic form, whereas the higher-degree corrections of the polyharmonic case are excluded by the
isometry requirement of Definition~\ref{Def:Intrinsic}. Finally, the affine conditioning ${\boldsymbol{p}}=(1,x_1,\dots,x_d)$ with
$N_0=d+1$ corresponds to $k_\gamma=1$, that is, to $1\le\gamma-\frac d2<2$; for the Laplacian
energy $\gamma=2$, this selects $d\in\{1,2\}$, whereas ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$ shrinks to $\Pi_0$ for
$d\in\{3,4\}$ and to $\{0\}$ for $d\ge5$. This differs from the classical Beppo--Levi setting \cite{Deny-Lions1954},
where the seminorm kernel is $\Pi_1$ in every dimension: the Beppo--Levi space is strictly larger
than the completion of ${\mathcal{S}}$ in high dimensions, and the difference is precisely the polynomial
directions that are not attainable from ${\mathcal{S}}$.

\medskip
\emph{Thin-plate setting ($\gamma=2$, $d=2$)}:
Here, ${\mathrm{B}}=\Delta^2$, $k_\gamma=1$, and we take ${\boldsymbol{p}}=(1,x_1,x_2)$ with $N_0=3$, so that
${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ consists of the test functions whose Fourier transform vanishes to second
order at the origin. The fundamental solution is the thin-plate function of Duchon
\cite{Duchon1977},
$g_2({\boldsymbol{x}})=\tfrac{1}{8\pi}\|{\boldsymbol{x}}\|^2\ln\|{\boldsymbol{x}}\|$, which instantiates the logarithmic
branch ($\gamma-\frac d2=1\in\mathbb{Z}_{\ge0}$), and the quadratic form on
${\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ is the classical thin-plate kernel form
\begin{align*}
\langle{\mathrm{A}}\varphi,\varphi\rangle
=\frac{1}{8\pi}\iint_{ \mathbb{R}^2\times \mathbb{R}^2}
\|{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{y}}\|^2\ln\|{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{y}}\|\,\varphi({\boldsymbol{x}})\varphi({\boldsymbol{y}})\,{\rm d}{\boldsymbol{x}}\,{\rm d}{\boldsymbol{y}},
\end{align*}
with conditional order $k_\gamma+1=2$. The space
$\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}=\dot H^{2}( \mathbb{R}^2)$ is the Beppo--Levi space of the thin-plate energy,
whose elements are functions modulo affine polynomials, while ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}$ consists of
the tempered distributions $u$ with $\hat u/\|{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\|^{2}\in L_2( \mathbb{R}^2)$, which
annihilate $\Pi_1$. The augmentation of Theorem~\ref{Thm:Augmentation}, with
${\mathrm{B}}_+=\Delta^2+\sum_{n=1}^{3}\phi_n\langle\cdot,\phi_n\rangle$, then yields the
concrete dual pair of Hilbert--Gelfand spaces
\begin{align*}
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}},
\qquad
{\mathcal{H}}'=\{f\in{\mathcal{S}}'( \mathbb{R}^2):\Delta f\in L_2( \mathbb{R}^2)\}
={\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}\oplus\Pi_1.
\end{align*}
<!-- end example-2 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
\label{Rem:Duchon}
The polyharmonic splines of Duchon \cite{Duchon1977} minimize $\|{\mathrm{D}}^m f\|^2_{L_2}$ over the
Beppo--Levi space $BL^m( \mathbb{R}^d)$ of Deny and Lions \cite{Deny-Lions1954}, whose seminorm has the
full algebraic kernel $\Pi_{m-1}$, in every dimension. By Example~\ref{Ex:FracLaplacian} with
$\gamma=m$, the attainable null space of the same energy is ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}=\Pi_{k_m}$ with
$k_m=\lfloor m-\tfrac d2\rfloor$, which coincides with $\Pi_{m-1}$ for $d\in\{1,2\}$ only. For
$d\ge3$, the polynomials of degree $k_m<j\le m-1$ have zero energy, but they are not limits of
test functions; in fact, ${\mathcal{S}}$ is not dense in $BL^m( \mathbb{R}^d)$ for any Hilbertian norm of the
form \eqref{Eq:AugmentedNormGeneral} that fixes the polynomial part, so that $BL^m( \mathbb{R}^d)$ is not a
Hilbert--Gelfand space, and Item~2 of Proposition~\ref{Prop:NativeUniqueness} rules it out as
the native space. Instead, $BL^m( \mathbb{R}^d)={\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}\oplus{\mathcal{G}}$, where the complement ${\mathcal{G}}$
is spanned by the polynomials of degree $k_m<j\le m-1$. Duchon's construction is correct within
$BL^m( \mathbb{R}^d)$, but the over-conditioning has practical consequences. First, it requires the data
sites for interpolation to be unisolvent for $\Pi_{m-1}$, whereas the energy only calls for $\Pi_{k_m}$: for $d=3$,
$m=2$ and the kernel $g_2({\boldsymbol{x}})\propto\|{\boldsymbol{x}}\|$, Duchon's interpolant needs four side conditions
and non-coplanar sites, while the spline of ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}$ exists and is unique for any set of
distinct points under the single condition $\sum_i a_i=0$. Second, the coefficients of the
polynomials in ${\mathcal{G}}$ are not regularized by the energy, whatever the value of the
regularization parameter; the data alone determine them. Third, ${\mathcal{G}}$ is annihilated by the
whole core ${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}={\mathrm{L}}^\ast(L_2)$, so that no functional of finite dual energy can
detect these components. The theory of radial basis functions \cite{Madych1990c,Wendland2005}
sidesteps the issue when it works with the minimal order $k_m+1$ of conditional positive
definiteness of $g_m$, which matches ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}$; the larger space $\Pi_{m-1}$ enters that
theory only through the Beppo--Levi spaces, where it is inherited from Duchon.
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- example-3: body_md -->
\label{Ex:ExpSplines}
Let $({\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{S}}')=({\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R}),{\mathcal{S}}'( \mathbb{R}))$ and let
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{L}}=P({\mathrm{D}})=a_{N_{\mathrm{tot}}}\prod_{n=1}^{N_{\mathrm r}}({\mathrm{D}}-\alpha_n\mathrm{Id})^{m_n},
\qquad
{\mathrm{D}}=\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d} x},
\end{align*}
where $P$ is an arbitrary polynomial with real coefficients, whose $N_{\mathrm r}$ distinct
roots $\alpha_n\in\mathbb{C}$ have multiplicities $m_n$. The total number of roots---that is,
the degree of $P$---is $N_{\mathrm{tot}}=\sum_{n=1}^{N_{\mathrm r}}m_n$. In contrast with the
fractional operators of Example~\ref{Ex:FracLaplacian}, ${\mathrm{L}}$ maps ${\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R})$ into itself; its
symbol is the polynomial $\hat L(\omega)=P(\mathrm{i}\omega)$, and the normal operator is
\begin{align*}
{\mathrm{B}}={\mathrm{L}}^\ast{\mathrm{L}}=P(-{\mathrm{D}})\,P({\mathrm{D}}),
\qquad
\widehat{{\mathrm{B}}\varphi}(\omega)=|P(\mathrm{i}\omega)|^2\,\hat\varphi(\omega).
\end{align*}

\medskip
\emph{Null spaces}:
The kernel of $P({\mathrm{D}})$ over $C^\infty( \mathbb{R})$ is the $N_{\mathrm{tot}}$-dimensional space of exponential
polynomials, spanned by the functions $x^k\mathrm{e}^{\alpha_nx}$ with $0\le k\le m_n-1$; since the
operator is hypoelliptic, this is also its kernel over ${\mathcal{D}}'( \mathbb{R})$. The tempered part of
this kernel retains exactly the roots on the imaginary axis, because $x^k\mathrm{e}^{\alpha_nx}$
grows exponentially when $\operatorname{Re}(\alpha_n)\neq0$. Writing $\alpha_n=\mathrm{i}\omega_n$ for the
imaginary roots, we therefore set
\begin{align*}
{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}
=\ker\big(P({\mathrm{D}}):{\mathcal{S}}'\to{\mathcal{S}}'\big)
=\mathrm{span}\{x^k\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_nx}:\ \alpha_n=\mathrm{i}\omega_n,\ 0\le k\le m_n-1\},
\end{align*}
with $N_0=\sum_{\alpha_n\in\mathrm{i} \mathbb{R}}m_n$; a real basis is obtained from $x^k\cos(\omega_nx)$ and
$x^k\sin(\omega_nx)$, the imaginary roots of a real polynomial arising in conjugate pairs.
Correspondingly,
\begin{align*}
{\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}
=\{\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}( \mathbb{R}):\
\hat\varphi^{(k)}(\omega_n)=0,\ 0\le k\le m_n-1,\ \alpha_n=\mathrm{i}\omega_n\},
\end{align*}
because
$\hat\varphi^{(k)}(\omega_n)=(-\mathrm{i})^k\langle x^k\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_nx},\varphi\rangle$.

\medskip
\emph{Verification via the Green inverse}:
The symbol $P(\mathrm{i}\omega)$ is a polynomial, so that it vanishes only on the finite set
$\{\omega_n\}$ of real zeros, each of order $m_n$; Conditions 1 and 2 of
Theorem~\ref{Thm:PracticalChecklist} follow. The dual energy is
\begin{align*}
Q(\varphi)
=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{ \mathbb{R}}\frac{|\hat\varphi(\omega)|^2}{|P(\mathrm{i}\omega)|^2}\,{\rm d}\omega,
\end{align*}
which always converges at infinity, because
$|P(\mathrm{i}\omega)|\sim|a_{N_{\mathrm{tot}}}|\,|\omega|^{N_{\mathrm{tot}}}$. Near a
real zero, $|P(\mathrm{i}\omega)|^2\asymp|\omega-\omega_n|^{2m_n}$: if $\hat\varphi$ vanishes at
$\omega_n$ to order $m_n$, then the integrand is bounded there; if the leading Taylor term of
$\hat\varphi$ at $\omega_n$ has order $j\le m_n-1$, then the radial exponent is
$2j-2m_n\le-2$ and the integral diverges. Hence $\{Q<\infty\}={\mathcal{S}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$; we note that
the borderline exponent $-1$ is never attained, because $2j-2m_n$ is even.

This is precisely the scenario of Corollary~\ref{Cor:GreenNormal}. The Green's function
$\rho_{\mathrm{B}}$ of the normal operator, with symbol $|P(\mathrm{i}\omega)|^{-2}$, is the classical
exponential-spline kernel (for $P(X)=X$, one recovers $\rho_{\mathrm{B}}(x)=-\tfrac12|x|$), and
${\mathrm{A}}=\rho_{\mathrm{B}}\ast\cdot$ is a two-sided Green inverse of ${\mathrm{B}}$. Item (iii) of the corollary
holds by the computation above, so that $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{B}})$ is a ${\boldsymbol{p}}$-conditional Riesz pair
with ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$: the conditioning by the tempered kernel of $P({\mathrm{D}})$ is
attainable and minimal. Condition 4 of Theorem~\ref{Thm:PracticalChecklist} then comes for
free, by the necessity part of Theorem~\ref{Thm:NormalPair} applied to the pair just
obtained, and Corollary~\ref{Cor:L2RealizationConditional} provides the $L_2$-realizations.

\medskip
\emph{Conditional inverse and native space}:
We set ${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}^\ast{\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$, where the biorthogonal family exists by
Lemma~\ref{Lem:Biorthogonal} and can be formed of Gabor--Hermite atoms
$x^k\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_nx}G(x)$, with $G$ a normalized Gaussian window. All the conclusions of
Theorems~\ref{Thm:NormalPair} and~\ref{Thm:PracticalChecklist} and of
Proposition~\ref{Prop:Factorization} hold: the homogeneous space
$\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}=\{f\in{\mathcal{S}}'( \mathbb{R}):P({\mathrm{D}})f\in L_2( \mathbb{R})\}/{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ carries the
exponential-spline energy $\|P({\mathrm{D}})f\|_{L_2}^2$, with concrete realization
${\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}$, while the native space itself is the augmented space
${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{L}}}={\mathcal{H}}'_{{\mathrm{A}}}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$ of \eqref{Eq:NativeSpace}; and
${\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathrm{A}}}\cong\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}\cong L_2( \mathbb{R})$ canonically,
with ${\mathrm{A}}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}={\mathrm{L}}^{-1}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}{\mathrm{L}}^{-1\ast}_{{\boldsymbol{\phi}}}$.

\medskip
\emph{Special cases}:
For $P(X)=X^m$, one recovers the polynomial-spline setting with
${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=\Pi_{m-1}$, in agreement with Example~\ref{Ex:FracLaplacian} for
$(\gamma,d)=(m,1)$. For $P(X)=X^2+\omega_0^2$, the conditioning is trigonometric:
${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=\mathrm{span}\{\cos(\omega_0x),$ $\sin(\omega_0x)\}$ with $N_0=2$. If $P$ has no
imaginary root (for instance $P(X)=X-a$ with $a\in \mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}$), then $N_0=0$: the pair
is an unconditioned compatible Riesz pair, and
$\dot{{\mathcal{H}}}_{{\mathrm{B}}}=\{f:P({\mathrm{D}})f\in L_2\}$ is a Sobolev-type space with a genuine norm.

\medskip
\emph{Two structural observations}:
First, only the imaginary-axis roots condition the pair: this is the one-dimensional
counterpart of the harmonic-polynomial discussion of Example~\ref{Ex:FracLaplacian}, with one
notable difference. Here, the attainable null space coincides with the \emph{entire} tempered
kernel, because each real zero of the symbol has order $m_n\ge1>d/2=\tfrac12$, so that all
the degrees $0\le k\le m_n-1$ pass the threshold; the gap between tempered and attainable
kernel elements is a genuinely multidimensional phenomenon. Second, the example extends
Proposition~\ref{Prop:MultiplierConstruction} to symbols whose zeros lie away from the
origin: each real zero $\omega_0$ of order $m$ contributes the modulated block
$\mathrm{span}\{x^k\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega_0x}:0\le k\le m-1\}$ to ${\mathcal{N}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}}$, in place of the
polynomial block attached to a zero at the origin.
<!-- end example-3 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
We prove $1\Rightarrow4$, $4\Rightarrow1\wedge2$ (which contains the converse part),
$2\Rightarrow1$, and $1\Leftrightarrow3$; we then establish the compatibility of the pairings
and the uniqueness of the pair.

\medskip
\emph{Step 1 ($1\Rightarrow4$)}:
For $\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}$, the functional
$\kappa^\ast\varphi:u\mapsto\langle\kappa u,\varphi\rangle$ is continuous on ${\mathcal{H}}$, so that
$\kappa^\ast\varphi\in{\mathcal{H}}'$. As $\kappa^\ast\varphi\in{\mathcal{H}}'$ for every $\varphi$, the family
$\{\langle\kappa u,\cdot\rangle:\|u\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}\le1\}$ is pointwise bounded on ${\mathcal{S}}$; since
${\mathcal{S}}$ is barrelled, the Banach--Steinhaus theorem \cite[Ch.~III]{Schaefer-Wolff1999} makes it
equicontinuous, so that
$\varphi\mapsto\|\kappa^\ast\varphi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}'}$ is a continuous seminorm on ${\mathcal{S}}$.
Moreover, $\kappa^\ast({\mathcal{S}})$ is dense in ${\mathcal{H}}'$, because its annihilator in ${\mathcal{H}}$ is
$\ker(\kappa)=\{0\}$, and $\kappa^\ast$ is the realization of ${\mathcal{S}}$ in
${\mathcal{H}}'$, so that $\|\kappa^\ast\varphi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}'}=\|\varphi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}'}$.

We now set ${\mathrm{A}}:=\jmath^\ast{\mathrm{J}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}\jmath$ and
${\mathrm{A}}^{-1}:=\kappa\,{\mathrm{J}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{-1}\kappa^\ast$, where
$\jmath^\ast:{\mathcal{H}}'\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ is the adjoint of $\jmath$. Both are continuous
${\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}'$, and, for $\varphi,\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$,
\begin{align*}
\langle{\mathrm{A}}\varphi,\psi\rangle
=\langle\jmath\varphi,\jmath\psi\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}},
\qquad
\langle{\mathrm{A}}^{-1}\varphi,\psi\rangle
=\langle\kappa^\ast\varphi,\kappa^\ast\psi\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}'}.
\end{align*}
Hence both operators are (symmetric and) positive-definite, with
$|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}=\|\varphi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}$ and
$|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}^{-1}}=\|\varphi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}'}$, which is the ``moreover'' identity of the
theorem. Under the identifications ${\mathcal{S}}\subset{\mathcal{H}}$, ${\mathcal{S}}\subset{\mathcal{H}}'$,
${\mathcal{H}}\subset{\mathcal{S}}'$, and ${\mathcal{H}}'\subset{\mathcal{S}}'$, they read
${\mathrm{A}}={\mathrm{J}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}|_{{\mathcal{S}}}$ and ${\mathrm{A}}^{-1}={\mathrm{J}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{-1}|_{{\mathcal{S}}}$. Since
${\mathcal{S}}$ is dense in ${\mathcal{H}}$ and in ${\mathcal{H}}'$ with matching norms, the isometric extensions
of ${\mathrm{A}}$ and ${\mathrm{A}}^{-1}$ are ${\mathrm{J}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}$ and ${\mathrm{J}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{-1}$, which are the
inverse of each other; the pair $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{A}}^{-1})$ is therefore a compatible Riesz pair,
which is Item 4.

Finally, the identity
$\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle
=\langle\kappa\jmath\psi,\varphi\rangle
=\langle\kappa^\ast\varphi,\jmath\psi\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}'\times{\mathcal{H}}}$
yields, by the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality and by density, the relations
\begin{align}
\label{Eq:CompatBound}
|\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle|
\le|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}^{-1}}\,|\psi|_{{\mathrm{A}}},
\qquad\varphi,\psi\in{\mathcal{S}},
\end{align}
\begin{align}
\label{Eq:CompatSharp}
|\psi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}
=\sup_{\varphi\neq0}
\frac{|\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle|}{|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}^{-1}}},
\qquad
|\varphi|_{{\mathrm{A}}^{-1}}
=\sup_{\psi\neq0}
\frac{|\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle|}{|\psi|_{{\mathrm{A}}}}.
\end{align}
The relations \eqref{Eq:CompatBound}--\eqref{Eq:CompatSharp} express, at the level of
${\mathcal{S}}$, that the extensions of ${\mathrm{A}}$ and ${\mathrm{A}}^{-1}$ are mutually inverse Riesz maps;
they constitute the operational content of Definition~\ref{Def:CompatibleRieszPair}.

\medskip
\emph{Step 2 ($4\Rightarrow1\wedge2$, and converse part)}:
Let $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{A}}^{-1})$ be a compatible Riesz pair, so that
\eqref{Eq:CompatBound}--\eqref{Eq:CompatSharp} hold. We form the completions
${\mathcal{H}}=\overline{({\mathcal{S}},|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{A}}})}$ and
${\mathcal{H}}'=\overline{({\mathcal{S}},|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{A}}^{-1}})}$ with ${\mathcal{S}}\overset{d}{\hookrightarrow} {\mathcal{H}}$ and ${\mathcal{S}}\overset{d}{\hookrightarrow} {\mathcal{H}}'$ by construction.

We now write ${\mathcal{H}}^\ast$ for the topological dual of ${\mathcal{H}}$. Each $\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$ defines the
functional $\ell_\psi=\langle\cdot,\psi\rangle$, which is $|\cdot|_{{\mathrm{A}}}$-continuous by
\eqref{Eq:CompatBound}, with $\|\ell_\psi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^\ast}=|\psi|_{{\mathrm{A}}^{-1}}$ by
\eqref{Eq:CompatSharp}. The map $\Lambda:\psi\mapsto\ell_\psi$ thus extends to a linear isometry
$\Lambda:{\mathcal{H}}'\to{\mathcal{H}}^\ast$, whose image is closed because ${\mathcal{H}}'$ is complete. To see that
$\Lambda$ is onto, we use the property that $\{\ell_\psi\}$ is norming for ${\mathcal{H}}$ (first identity of
\eqref{Eq:CompatSharp}): if $\nu=\langle\cdot,u\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}}\in{\mathcal{H}}^\ast$ is orthogonal to every
$\ell_\psi$, then $\ell_\psi(u)=\langle u,\psi\rangle=0$ for all $\psi\in{\mathcal{S}}$, so that $u=0$ and
hence $\nu=0$. The image $\Lambda({\mathcal{H}}')$ is therefore dense as well as closed, hence all of
${\mathcal{H}}^\ast$; consequently ${\mathcal{H}}'={\mathcal{H}}^\ast$, and the pairing
$\langle f,g\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}'\times{\mathcal{H}}}$ extends the ${\mathcal{S}}$--${\mathcal{S}}'$ pairing.

It remains to realize the spaces in ${\mathcal{S}}'$. The inclusion $\jmath:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{H}}$ is continuous
with dense range, so its transpose $\jmath^\ast:{\mathcal{H}}^\ast\to{\mathcal{S}}'$ is injective and
weak-$\ast$ continuous, and it extends $\iota$. Through ${\mathcal{H}}'={\mathcal{H}}^\ast$, this realizes
${\mathcal{H}}'$ as a subspace of ${\mathcal{S}}'$; that is, ${\mathcal{S}}\overset{d}{\hookrightarrow}{\mathcal{H}}'\overset{}{\hookrightarrow}{\mathcal{S}}'$. By the symmetry
of \eqref{Eq:CompatBound}--\eqref{Eq:CompatSharp} in $({\mathrm{A}},{\mathrm{A}}^{-1})$, the same argument gives
